Fostering Is In My Blood

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

I remember when my mom brought home our first fosters. I was in fifth grade, and when my twin sister, Becca, and I came home from school, our mom walked us over to a box with three little neonatal kittens, just days old: Amelia, Angel, and Buddy. With closed eyes and little bodies running purely on instinct, they wriggled over one another, crying out to be fed. I watched as my mom gently picked them up, and as she did, she turned to us, smiling, and said, âtheyâre your responsibilityâ.

I was flooded with excitement and fear all at once. I knew nothing about caring for something so small. I watched in awe as my mom bottle-fed them one by one. The meowing subsided, and their little ears wiggled as they latched on and ate until their tiny bellies were full. The lesson had begun regardless of my fears. But feeding, I quickly learned, was just step one.

Soon after, we were stimulating each kitten to go potty, testing the temperature of their enclosure, wiping them clean, and getting them settled in for a post-bottle nap. So much happened so quickly, and they were so small and fragile. I told my mom I was nervous that I wouldnât be able to do what she did. She laughed and, with a grin, informed me I would have plenty of time to learn: Amelia, Angel, and Buddy would need to eat every 2 hours, even throughout the night. So we had lots of chances to practice and retain all the new skills she was showing us.

Over the next few weeks, we watched them grow and grow until they were ready to find their forever homes. As adoption day approached, it was incredibly hard. My sister and I begged our mom to let us keep the kittens. After all, we had raised them, and our love for them was undeniable. But she assured us that this was a good thing; letting them go meant we could help more animals in need. In the moment, however, I didnât understand. Instead, I cried myself to sleep holding the kittens. My kittens.

Then the day came, and even though the adopters were my friends, I bristled when they knocked at the door. But as they came in and met Amelia, Angel and Buddy, something magical happened: I watched my friends fall in love with them just like I had. They giggled at the clumsy way Buddy walked. Their hearts melted as they watched the girls cuddle in the sun. I was still scared to let the kittens go, but now part of me was excited, too. My friends saw them how I saw them. I had to trust that the little babies that we raised were on to bigger and better things. Things that would allow us to welcome another baby in need. But still, my heart broke - I had fallen in love with each of them, and saying goodbye terrified me.

There wasnât much time after they were adopted to worry about my fears, though. Before I knew it, we said hello to our next foster: a skittish and energetic 5-week-old boy we called Kipper. I thought maybe this time Iâd try to guard my heart, but Kipper stole it anyway. His silly antics and never-ending supply of energy made it impossible not to fall in love with him, too. Part of me hoped that maybe this time, Kipper could stay. But my mom held firm, and a few weeks later another friend came to us to adopt. I watched it play out all over again. I smiled as they swooned over this little precious kitten and all his quirks, just like I had.

This time, though I was still heartbroken, I felt a sense of understanding settle in, too. I remember thinking to myself, as Kipper fell asleep, purring, in my friendâs arms, âI wonder who we will get to help next?â

It didnât take long for that question to get its answer: Four tiny black-and-white kittens found in a hot tub and brought to our doorstep. Three were friendly; one, wellâ¦he was anything but! Cleo, Leo, and Brandy settled in beautifully, but Bindy just needed more.

More time, more attention, more understanding.

I felt drawn to him, and while my twin tended to the others, I found myself focused on Bindy. He would hide, hiss, spit, and bite. But really, he was just a little thing in a big world, and he was terrified. I worked with him every day, and slowly but surely, he made progress. When adoption day came around, however, we decided Bindy wasnât ready yet. He stayed behind to gain more confidence and, in time, became our first foster-fail.

Sometimes it was best for a foster to be adopted, but sometimes, they were already home.

Bindy eventually became the most loving cat I ever knew. He was my soul cat, and my best friend. And even though he stayed, I didnât avoid the heartbreak that came with saying goodbye; it just came many, many years later when old age finally took him. I learned a second lesson from Bindy then: there is no love without loss and, despite the pain, love would always be worth it.

So I continued to dive in, foster after foster. Heartbreak after heartbreak. Miracle after miracle. And it was always worth it; just like my mom said it would be. Iâm no longer the little girl learning from my mother; those lessons are now a part of me. Theyâre one of the things she left behind after a lifetime of love. My heart broke saying goodbye to her, too, but her teachings gave me a life thatâs filled to the brim with purpose. And paired with a passion inspired by my first foster fail, Bindy, I quickly developed a soft spot for the âspicyâ foster cats; and thatâs what I do now.

Currently, a euthanasia-list survivor is calling my home their safe haven. Just like Bindy, Simon wasterrified and aggressive, and he almost paid for it with his life. While Iâve been fostering him, however, Iâve gotten to know a completely different cat than the one in the shelter. A cat thatâs full of love and silly antics, whoâs eager to hold a conversation and meow for your attention. A cat who just needed a chance.

When his adoption day comes, I know my heart will break again; it always does. Itâs the price we pay for loving something, and Iâll pay that price over and over again because it means I get to watch someone adore him in all the ways I have, and more. The internet will go into an uproar demanding I keep him, but my momâs voice will sound clearly in my ear and keep me grounded:

âItâs a good thing. Letting them go means we can help others in need.â

So I'll say goodbye, knowing another Simon, Bindy, or Kipper is waiting for their chance to be loved. And as I walk back into the shelter, the lesson I began learning all those years ago in fifth grade remains true: Fostering Saves Lives.

As a third-generation animal rescuer, Rachael Rae Robertson has truly never had a moment where her life didnât revolve around animals â and she wouldnât have it any other way. Her grandmother had a farm for retired racetrack horses and farm animals, her aunt worked in wildlife rehabilitation, and her mom focused on pets like bunnies, dogs, birds, and cats. Though all three of these inspirational women are no longer here with us, their lessons remain. Now, Rachael hopes to share the gifts they gave her and her twin sister, Becca, with the world. With over 450k followers on TikTok and 190k on Instagram, Rachael documents her foster animals and their journeys, shedding light on the beautiful ups and downs of fostering while sharing the lessons sheâs gathered from her family, far and wide. She hopes to inspire others to dip their toes into the world of rescue, too, whether itâs through fostering, volunteering, or activism. In her free time, she can be found in her happy place â aka with her tetrad of tabbies and senior pup!

Gary Neville tells Mauricio Pochettino to reject Manchester United unless two signings are made

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Gary Neville has told Mauricio Pochettino to reject the Manchester United job unless the club promise to sign two defenders in the January transfer window.

With United confirming the sacking of Michael Carrick on Thursday morning, Pochettino is now expected to take over at Old Trafford.

After Sunday’s 4-4 draw at home to Aston Villa, United sit 18th in the Premier League table after 10 games, while they are also out of the Carabao Cup following a defeat to Brighton in September.

Pochettino, who has a $10 million (£7.44m) release clause in his contract with the United States Soccer Federation, has already reached an agreement with United over a move to Old Trafford.

However, Neville believes the former Tottenham and Chelsea manager should turn down United unless the club’s hierarchy can issue guarantees over spending in the next two transfer windows.

‘I’ve actually asked for Mauricio Pochettino to be the manager of Manchester United probably for seven or eight years and he’s finally going to get it, a bit late in the day for me but he’s finally going to get it,’ Neville said on Stick to Football.

‘He’s going to be sat in the Anfield dugout a week on Sunday, do we think that this is an appointment that will take Manchester United back to a level whereby over three or five years he can keep his job and he can deliver what Klopp did at Liverpool or what Arteta did at Arsenal where there’s that gradual improvement, that feeling you’re on a journey where something can happen?

‘If I was Mauricio Pochettino now, I would be literally protecting myself up to the high heaven. He’ll do that with a payoff, so do that in your contract.

‘But secondly, he needs two defenders in January. So he’s got to insist that he gets two defenders in January.

‘He’s got to insist that he gets a minimum budget next summer for the transfer window to be able to turn this around. He’s got to insist that he gets a budget that follows.

Everyone Is Talking About Wicker's Sexy Parts, But I Was Caught Off Guard By Its Emotional Depth

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

After Watching The Invite As A Divorcee, I Have A Take On The Ending

One Big Thing That's Keeping Me From Thinking Wild Horse Nine Is One Of This Year's Best

There Are Like A Dozen WTF Moments In The Elizabeth Holmes Documentary, But One Is Haunting Me

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The romance is great, but the emotional depth is better.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Wicker has been advertised as an extremely sexually charged movie with a hot man made of wicker. The film doesn’t shy away from sex and sexuality. It has a few very fun sex scenes that a lot of hard work went into. I wasn’t sure what to expect going into Wicker beyond some raunchiness.

I was delighted to discover it's far more complex than it's promoted. It touches upon topics of self-esteem in really haunting and interesting ways.

Warning: Wicker spoilers are ahead. Proceed with caution.

Fisherwoman (Olivia Colman) has always been an outcast in her town. They tease and treat her as if her only purpose is to sell them fish. She has no real friends and not much of a romantic prospect. This led the Fisherwoman to accept a lonely life. She finally decides to change that, but the scars remain from being told that she doesn’t deserve good things, especially a man who loves her.

The Fisherwoman has a lot of trauma and self-esteem issues from years of being told by society that she’s not worthy of happiness. However, she isn’t the only one with society-based scars. All the women in town have them. This is why the Wicker Husband (Alexander Skarsgård) is so attractive to many women. He doesn’t operate with the same rules of this society.

He loves deeply and purely, without constraining how his wife should live. Sadly, this nonconforming spirit also makes the Fisherwoman doubt his love and fidelity. She has seen the men of this society. She finds it hard to believe that she has one who respects and loves her.

I left Wicker very annoyed with the Fisherwoman. She almost killed her husband because of a lie. Additionally, she refused to believe him, even though he has only ever been honest. It’s also clear that he only exists for her. I was shouting, “Be reasonable, woman!” as she behaved in unreasonable ways.

However, then I had to think about how many women and men let their lack of self-esteem or past trauma ruin good things in their lives. Many relationships have been destroyed because one or both people in them let their insecurities grow and create bigger problems. The Fisherwoman is frustrating, but she’s very human.

I really enjoyed the Wicker ending because everyone got what they deserved. The townsfolk got scammed and traumatized. The Butcher (Phil Daniels ) got murdered, as the worst husband of them all, and the Fisherwoman and Wicker Husband left with a new child.

It was only after the Fisherwoman could let go of her insecurities and her awful town that she could have a happy ending. Wicker is about letting love into your life, even when you don’t think you deserve it.

The Fisherwoman creates a husband who loves her completely, but she also learns to love herself in the process. That’s the real message. Find love within yourself, and the right person may find you, or you will at least love yourself enough to feel fulfilled with or without a partner. Wicker is definitely worth seeing in a theater full of women, or alone, where you can meditate on its messages.

Either way, come for the sexy parts, stay for the self-love.

Spent most of my life in various parts of Illinois, including attending college in Evanston. I have been a life long lover of pop culture, especially television, turned that passion into writing about all things entertainment related. When I'm not writing about pop culture, I can be found channeling Gordon Ramsay by kicking people out the kitchen.

What's Next For Bitcoin (BTC)? CME FedWatch Shows 100% Odds of a December Fed Rate Hike

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

CME FedWatch puts the implied chance of a Fed rate hike at 100% for the December 9 meeting, according to the November 12 snapshot. This is another source of pressure on Bitcoin, and there is a good chance the market is going to face its full effect.

Bitcoin and the cryptocurrency market in general aren't set in stone and are highly affected by the performance of traditional markets and the global economy. A rate hike directly affects the desire of investors to expose themselves to riskier assets like crypto.

Bitcoin has been going down rapidly even without the additional source of pressure from the Fed. The current BTC price drop appears to be mostly fueled by insufficient compensation for the risk: Bitcoin and crypto in general aren't as attractive as tech or AI stocks, so it is far better to get exposed to the latter ones.

That is the bearish reading here. FedWatch reflects futures pricing, so the hike is already expected rather than guaranteed to become a fresh shock.

Bitcoin is trading at $72,744, below all the moving averages shown on the daily chart. A drop below $70,000 shouldn't necessarily trigger any substantial surge of selling pressure, considering how stretched the decline already looks.

The imbalance of inflows and outflows is still staggering in this reading of the market. The concern is that almost every top-15 crypto asset remains in endurance mode, facing persistent outflows fueled by security concerns, a retreat in investor sentiment and a lack of substantial recovery prospects.

This view draws on Coinglass, CryptoQuant and SosoValue, which track derivatives, on-chain activity and ETF behavior respectively. The interpretation is that all three fields show outflows from digital assets alongside a growing supply of stablecoins.

A full-blown recovery of the cryptocurrency market would need new catalysts and use cases. A potential burst of the AI bubble, with substantial losses for the largest market players, might push them towards digital assets. A transition is possible, but it's not guaranteed by any means.

The confirmation of a market recovery would only come if we witness higher highs and constant inflows into institutional products like ETFs.

Kim Kardashian Plays Nurse in One of Louis Vuitton’s Rarest Runway Looks

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Kim Kardashian has been raiding the fashion archives with extraordinary enthusiasm of late, but her latest vintage pull comes with a health warning. Enter: a very rare, almost 20-year-old Louis Vuitton nurse uniform, complete with a monogrammed lace face mask, personalized cap, and coordinating accessories. The look, from Marc Jacobs’s spring 2008 collection for the house, is a heart-palpitating piece of runway history.

The ensemble was sourced by stylist Law Roach from the personal archive of Lauren Pyes, a Boston-based Harvard University employee and avid collector of Jacobs-era Louis Vuitton, whose extraordinary collection Vogue first explored last year. Pyes has spent years scouring resale platforms, boutiques, and obscure corners of the internet for some of the designer’s most coveted creations, amassing what she affectionately calls the “Marchive.”

“Law reached out to me a few months ago and was interested in pieces of the Marchive," Pyes tells Vogue. “Suffice to say, I was thrilled to loan them to him.” When Roach first contacted her, she could scarcely believe it was him. “I kept toggling back and forth to make sure it wasn’t a spam account!” she recalls. “Although he has a larger-than-life public persona, Law is deeply kind and gracious in addition to being the visionary he is rightly lauded as. I have so much respect for his dedication to telling stories through fashion and his commitment to finding the pieces that do exactly that.”

And few collections have quite as much storytelling potential as Louis Vuitton’s spring 2008 offering. Presented in Paris in October 2007, the show opened with a procession of supermodels—including Naomi Campbell, Stephanie Seymour, Eva Herzigova, and Nadja Auermann—dressed as nurses in sheer, pastel-colored uniforms, black lace masks, and white caps. The inspiration was the American artist Richard Prince, whose provocative Nurse Paintings informed the collection and accompanying collaboration with the house. Reviewing the collection at the time, Vogue’s Sarah Mower described it as a “typical Jacobs coup of multidimensional, referential, and self-referential significance,” and a “hilariously kinky parade” that “reflected Jacobs’s way of walking the line between creative freedom and commercial innovation.” It was one of Jacobs’s most deviant, deliciously strange runway spectacles.

The shoes from Louis Vuitton spring 2028.

The full look, as sourced by Lauren Pyes.

Kim Kardashian’s interpretation was remarkably faithful to the original. She wore the sheer, short-sleeved white nurse’s coat cinched at the waist over a delicate, peach-colored silk dress, with white stockings and peep-toe heels. A black lace Louis Vuitton face mask covered her mouth, while a white nurse’s cap (commissioned by Roach and reimagined by legendary milliner Stephen Jones) bearing a red “K” sat atop her sleek dark hair. In her arms was another treasure from the collection: an Aquarelle Papillon bag. The shoes also hailed from spring 2008.

Bringing together such a complete ensemble was no small undertaking. “I sourced these pieces from all over—including platforms like Poshmark and Vestiaire Collective—for over a year,” Pyes explains. The dresses have become a particular obsession: she has owned four, including a pink silk satin version that remains in her permanent collection. Vogue actually purchased another of the orange dresses—the same color Kardashian wore—from Pyes a little over a year ago. But the real rarity is the mask and overcoat, which Pyes considers among her most treasured finds. Earlier this year, the ensemble was exhibited at the Savannah College of Art and Design (SCAD), where it even caught the attention of one of its original creators.

“I had it exhibited at SCAD back in April, and Stephen Jones—who made it in collaboration with Louis Vuitton—was apparently surprised that it even existed!” Pyes says of the mask. “If the person who made it was in disbelief by seeing it, I guess that means it is pretty rare!”

Kardashian and Roach have been on a formidable archival streak throughout the All’s Fair press tour, resurrecting everything from ’90s Paco Rabanne couture to vintage Dolce & Gabbana and Jean Paul Gaultier-era Hermès. The Louis Vuitton nurse look is their most playful fashion deep dive yet.

And for Pyes, it’s precisely that irreverence that makes the Jacobs years so compelling. She points to the extraordinary creative team behind the collection, including Peter Copping, then head of womenswear and now creative director of Lanvin, and Camille Miceli, currently at the helm of Pucci. “This collection and these pieces are so special because they highlight what Marc and his team did so well—seamlessly integrating fashion and art into a cohesive and powerful narrative,” she says. She remembers the supermodel casting, the nurse uniforms, and even Jacobs taking his final bow beside a custom Vuitton trunk playing an episode of SpongeBob SquarePants. “There are so many elements that were unexpected and genuine in a pre-social media age.”

Pyes, of course, has continued to do some killer sourcing. She’s recently completed a “trifecta of accessories” from the Louis Vuitton fall 2011 collection, better known as the “Fetish Collection.” This includes a shearling porter hat, mask hair accessory, and a patent leather bib. On the non-vintage side of things? “I am champing at the bit to receive the custom order I put in from Marc’s Spring 2027 collection,” she shares. “After more than 40 years, he continues to create incredible fashion and I feel fortunate to be adding this ensemble to the Marchive!”

Whether Kim’s look was a Halloween latergram, another cheeky press tour outing, or just a moment of play inside an impressive archive, for now, is anyone’s guess. Whatever the occasion, Pyes is delighted to see the collection enjoying a new life in the public imagination. Nearly two decades after its debut, Jacobs’s peculiar, provocative vision remains as potent as ever. “These clothes are not just beautiful and special,” she says, “but also consequential. This collection walked almost 20 years ago and yet continues to figure so largely in pop culture and the imagination of fashion lovers.” Nurse Kardashia, the defibrillator please!

There’s also a chance that some other Kasane Teto Outfit may be added to the item shop in the future. This is usually the case for most artists, so keep an eye out on the shop for an additional style that can be bought using V-Bucks.

The Kasane Teto Twindrill Diva music pass runs from Nov. 12, 2026 to Jan. 20, 2027. This means you have about two and a half months to decide whether you want to purchase and work through it.

India's Financial Crimes Agency Searches CoinDCX, Delta Exchange Over Offshore Trades

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

India's Financial Crimes Agency Searches CoinDCX, Delta Exchange Over Offshore Trades

India's Enforcement Directorate has searched three premises in Bengaluru linked to crypto exchanges CoinDCX and Delta Exchange, alleging the platforms carried out unauthorized cross-border transactions worth hundreds of millions of dollars through crypto derivatives and tokenized assets.

The searches were carried out on November 4 and 5 under India's Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), the agency, India's main financial crimes investigator, said in a statement dated November 6, which it shared on X on Thursday.

ईडी, बेंगलुरु ने क्रिप्टो टोकन डेरिवेटिव एवं वास्तविक विश्व परिसंपत्ति (आरडब्ल्यूए) टोकन से जुड़े बड़े पैमाने पर अनधिकृत सीमा-पार वित्तीय लेन-देन के मामले में फेमा, 1999 के अंतर्गत मेसर्स नेब्लियो टेक्नोलॉजीज प्राइवेट लिमिटेड (“कॉइनडीसीएक्स”) तथा मेसर्स एक्सेलियम टेक्नोलॉजीज… pic.twitter.com/vXwMU5uCTj

The investigation targets Neblio Technologies, which operates CoinDCX, and Excelium Technologies, which runs Delta Exchange. Both platforms offered futures and options on crypto tokens and openly advertised trading in tokenized shares of foreign companies, despite not being authorized to do so, according to the agency.

The Enforcement Directorate alleged that CoinDCX used stablecoins such as USDT to carry out trades for its users on foreign crypto exchanges, including Binance, KuCoin, Bybit and Gate.io, bypassing India's framework for sending money abroad.

When users deposited rupees and placed spot or derivatives orders, CoinDCX converted the funds into USDT and opened matching positions on the foreign exchanges, the agency said. The trades ran through accounts held in the name of a Costa Rica-based group entity, which it described as a shell entity, and were carried out directly from CoinDCX through APIs.

Trades in tokenized shares of companies such as Apple, Google and Tesla were in fact derivatives positions opened on decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, according to the agency.

The Enforcement Directorate alleged that the cross-border transactions in each of these areas ran to hundreds of millions of dollars. It also said CoinDCX holds crypto assets in enterprise accounts on foreign exchanges, and has paid tens of millions of dollars in fees to those platforms in USDT, in breach of FEMA.

The agency said a large share of Delta Exchange's derivatives trading was executed through CoinDCX, and that Delta Exchange had transferred tens of millions of dollars to its overseas group entities, which is under investigation. Their fintech products also appear to contravene India's securities and central banking laws, it said, adding that its investigation is ongoing.

CoinDCX said in a tweet on November 6 that Enforcement Directorate officials had visited its Bengaluru office "as part of a routine enquiry" on November 4, and that it had fully cooperated. The exchange said it operates "in strict compliance with all applicable Indian laws, including FEMA," and adheres to its KYC, anti-money laundering and reporting obligations as an entity registered with India's Financial Intelligence Unit. Trading, deposits and withdrawals on the platform remain fully operational, it added.

Officials from the Enforcement Directorate visited our Bengaluru office as part of a routine enquiry on 4th November. We fully cooperated with the authorities and provided all necessary information and assistance.

As an entity registered with FIU-IND, we regularly…

Delta Exchange said in its own tweet on November 6 that it had cooperated fully with officials, and that its business "does not involve any cross-border transfer of VDAs in any form or manner," using India's term for crypto assets. It said it runs a dedicated rupee-settled exchange for Indian users only, has been registered with India's Financial Intelligence Unit since 2023, and that trading, deposits and withdrawals were functioning normally.

The investigation adds to a difficult stretch for CoinDCX, one of India's largest crypto exchanges. Last month, it confirmed a $16 million loss from retired wallets, days after co-founder and CEO Sumit Gupta called reports of a breach a "false alarm." That followed a roughly $44 million exploit in July 2025.

Coinbase, which has invested in CoinDCX since 2020, won approval from India's competition regulator to increase its minority stake in the exchange's parent company.

‘Pregnancy does not mean game over’: meet the footballers challenging the myths | Moving the Goalposts

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Claire Emslie and Emma Kelly are among those taking advantage of new research and guidelines for pregnant players

Claire Emslie wasn’t expecting to be able to run at 81% of her max speed at 32 weeks pregnant.

Emslie, a Scotland international who also plays as a forward for Angel City FC, documented her journey through pregnancy and football training on social media and is part of a growing number of professional female footballers who are getting pregnant in their athletic prime and approaching motherhood with a fresh mindset. Rather than marking the end of a career (notably, Millie Bright announced her pregnancy in September, months after having retired), pregnancy can mark both a continuation and a renewal.

“I think it’s becoming more common. You’re seeing it more often, especially in America,” says 32-year-old Emslie, who trained at high intensity throughout her pregnancy, with a three-person team looking after her. She gave birth to her son, Jamie, in December 2025 and returned to competitive action by May 2026; scoring her first goal since coming back for Scotland in October, and her first club goal this month. “It’s amazing what the body can do. I debunked a lot of myths that I’d heard about being pregnant.”

For many years, says the physiotherapist and Women’s Football Hub founder Carolyn Kent, there was huge caution around high-impact sports and pregnancy. The fear was that overexertion and physical contact – a heart rate going too high, a bad fall, a mistimed kick to the abdomen – could cause miscarriage. But slowly this is changing. In July Fifa launched its Stay in Play guidelines and research, which offer a groundbreaking framework for decision-making around football participation during pregnancy.

“I was always told that once you hit that first trimester, 12 weeks, all contact stops. But actually they’re now saying, no, we need to be a little bit more nuanced than that,” says Kent. The research suggests that while that timeframe should still be considered the loose recommended cut-off point for full-contact football (due to the uterus growing over the pubic bone and offering less protection for the growing foetus in the second trimester), low-contact and modified training can continue in the absence of any medical reason for it not to.

“Pregnancy does not mean game over,” adds Kent. “It does not mean that ‘OK, I’ve got to automatically stop’. That again feeds into this narrative that females are fragile. Your body’s amazing.”

Up to 2021, only 6% of sports science research was done on women, which means gaps are still being filled in. But as the women’s game continues to grow, this is happening at speed, says Sinéad Dufour, a pelvic health physiotherapist and academic who was one of the researchers who developed the Stay in Play guidelines. She gives Fifa credit for recognising the development of the women’s game – its popularity and intensity has soared in the past 10 years with more than six million women and girls playing football in England alone.

“More and more players are now staying through until they’re having families … And they put all this money into research,” she says. They have identified “clear cut” physical and mental health benefits for women to keep up with football, including a lower injury risk postpartum.

“There’s always been a notion that women just aren’t strong and robust the way we actually are, and now the science is proving otherwise,” Dufour says. “I do think some of it relates to the foetus. That’s going to supersede what’s actually good for the woman, right?”

Some of the main considerations players should have, whether professional or amateur, as to whether they should keep playing and training throughout pregnancy, relate to pain, pelvic floor dysfunction, and motor control and movement, she adds. But the decision whether or not to continue playing during pregnancy is ultimately personal.

When Emma Kelly, who plays for Newcastle United, unexpectedly became pregnant earlier this year at the age of 29, she was anxious. She had never played with a pregnant teammate before and she had been out for much of the season due to injury. How isolating would the experience be? Was it going to jeopardise her career? “I was questioning whether this is the right time,” she says. “It’s not that I’m ungrateful but it’s just finding it hard being out of the team again and not being able to play the sport that I love.”

Though she continued to train, she and her partner decided she would stop contact football immediately, prior to 12 weeks, as “football is so unpredictable”, she says. As she didn’t want to announce her pregnancy until she was into the second trimester, in the interim she told her teammates that she was unwell. Like Emslie she has been documenting her training with the encouragement of her club and feels as if she is noticing more and more athletes doing the same. “I want to continue as long as my body allows me to,” she says. Her club has been “extremely supportive” with specialised training, physio and equipment.

But for all the newfound positivity around pre-and postpartum for footballers, Emslie says candidly, there are some hard truths that players have to face: namely that pregnancy is a sacrifice. The pitch is their stage and no player wants to be relegated to the role of understudy, relearning how to bend their body to their will. “If I wasn’t worried about [fertility], I wouldn’t have gotten pregnant till I was retired,” she says. “I could have had a full football career.”

The other is that football is a business. “You’re used as a player,” she says bluntly. “As soon as you turn a certain age, or if you’ve got a kid, they look at you different … As a player, you’re wanted until you’re not.” Though she made it clear she wasn’t talking about her own club, which was very supportive of her pregnancy and return, at the time of interview she was two months away from her contract ending with Angel City but had no news of a new deal being offered.

Even so, Dufour says, the body works hard during pregnancy; aerobic capacity is pushed. “The biggest stress test you’re ever going to go through is pregnancy,” she says. Her data shows that some women will achieve personal bests on their return. In short pregnancy could be a secret superpower. In an industry as capitalistic, brutal and data-driven as football, that’s an important fact.

Skiers, Your Eyes Need More Protection Than You Think. Here’s What Doctors Say.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Catie Evans had been skiing for days along the Continental Divide in Colorado when she woke up with swollen eyes.

After logging multiple 12-hour days back-to-back, all while wearing glacier glasses, the avid backcountry skier figured she was just tired. But as she skinned toward Dead Dog Couloir on 14,267-foot Torrey’s Peak, she remembers, “I started to feel like somebody had thrown sand in my eyes.”

Eventually, with her eyes watering, she had to ski down with one eye covered, squinting out of the other with her friends guiding her.

Later, Evans tried to troubleshoot. Had she damaged her glasses without realizing it? Did she miss news of a recall? Or, throughout the multi-day trip, had she taken them off more than she thought, slowly racking up UV exposure in the process?

She’s still not sure. But, she’s not the only skier to experience snow blindness, and her experience illustrates the importance of eye protection at high altitude. While we often discuss features like fit, contrast, and style when it comes to choosing goggles, sometimes we don’t spend as much time thinking about what they’re protecting us from. So, we called up a few eye doctors to ask all of your burning questions about eye safety on the slopes.

While you might associate sun damage with summer days, skiers are constantly enjoying a double-dose of UV radiation thanks to the strength of the sun above combined with the reflective snow below. And, that exposure increases by roughly 4 to 5 percent for every 1,000-foot increase in elevation thanks to the gradually-thinning atmosphere.

Never let temperature or overcast conditions fool you; those invisible rays are always shining down, and they’ll pierce right through the clouds into your unsuspecting eyeballs.

We Tested 29 Pairs of Goggles. These are the 10 Best for the 2026-’27 Ski Season.

“Whenever we’re outside, (UV) rays can damage the surface of the eye as well as the cornea and lens,”  explained Dr. Becky Leenheer, spokesperson for the American Academy of Ophthalmology spokesperson, an assistant professor at the University of New Mexico, and an ophthalmologist herself.

Children, she said, are especially vulnerable. “Over time, cumulative UV exposure raises the risk of cataracts and of growths on the eye’s surface.”

Dr. Thomas Litzinger, an avid skier and ophthalmologist at ReVision Lasik and Cataract Surgery in Ohio, noted that the scariest potential consequence of continued exposure is cancers of the eye or the skin around it.

These conditions develop gradually, Leenheer said, but you shouldn’t ignore short-term discomfort either. Issues like photokeratitis (snow blindness), which is basically a burn to the corneal surface, hurts like heck and can contribute to further problems, though you may not feel them developing in the moment. Going forward, they’re a sign to protect your eyes better next time.

Here’s the good news: Almost any pair of goggles on the market should protect you from these risks. To be sure, look for claims like “100% UV (or UVA and UVB) Protection” or “UV400.”

One caveat is that goggles are not regulated in the United States. While any reputable company should be safe, we make no promises about the $20 goggles you found on Amazon. Litzinger added that the European Union does regulate goggles, and the label CE (Conformité Européenne) denotes that a pair meets EU standards.

Beyond UV protection, he explained, choosing goggles comes down to preference, or safety in the sense that better visual quality and field-of-view may help you avoid other accidents.

He notes that one common misconception is conflating UV protection with Visible Light Transmission (VLT). This factor determines how much brightness your goggles let in, but has no bearing on UV protection.

“There is no correlation between darker lenses and higher UV blocking,” added Dr. Cameron Cutler, an optometrist at Summit Vision in Frisco, Colorado. “Tint color does not matter, buy what you like!”

Features such as different tints, anti-fog measures, and lens shapes may promise improvements when it comes to comfort and visual quality, but they also don’t impact sun exposure itself.

Litzinger added that some skiers mistake glare complications for snow blindness, but this is another misconception. Though glare can be painful, or even reduce one’s ability to see, the cause is excessive brightness and likely choosing the wrong VLT for the conditions, not UV exposure.

As unpleasant as glare is, you can rest assured you’re protected from a UV standpoint with quality goggles.

Some companies do try to mitigate this issue by offering tinted, high-contrast or polarized lenses for a more comfortable ride. But be aware the latter poses a trade-off many skiers opt against, Cutler noted, as it can hide dangerous icy patches as well as glare.

Overall, Litzinger said, “It’s pretty important as skiers and riders to match your lens to the conditions to really discern contrast and terrain.” Additionally, Leenheer said, skiers should make sure their goggles are impact-resistant and fit snugly to protect you from wind, snow, and excess sunlight.

But, choosing the wrong ones (provided they have UV protection) won’t lead to any long-term eye damage, and neither will opting for budget goggles. “On the UV front, cheap and expensive are on the same playing field,” Litzinger said, adding that expensive goggles may distinguish themselves in other ways including fit, durability, and optical quality.

While some glasses and contacts block UV rays too, Cutler noted that full-coverage goggles offer the most protection for your eyes, as well as the sensitive skin around them.

That said, you’re only protected if you’re wearing them.

Plenty of eye damage occurs when skiers stop for lunch or a beer, take off the Oakleys, and expose their eyes to all those UV rays they’ve been avoiding all day, Cutler noted. So, even once you’ve taken off your skis, be aware of your environment and ready to rock eye protection for as long as you’re outdoors.

Review: Apple's M6 iMac is pricey, but it's still a good choice for a clean desk

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Apple’s M6 keeps the iMac current without shaking things up much.

The Apple Silicon iMac got its fourth refresh this month.

Every other Apple Silicon Mac has found a way to move forward over the last half-decade—we’ve gotten a more Pro design for the MacBook Pro, a mini-er design for the Mac mini, and a Mac Studio that has finally made Apple admit it doesn’t want to make the Mac Pro anymore.

But the Apple Silicon iMac has always been more interested in looking backward. It’s the thin, screen-on-a-stand iMac design by way of the original Bondi Blue G3 iMac. It’s a clean and pretty desktop computer, one I see all the time in small offices and on TV shows that depict small offices. We’re long past the days when it was Apple’s primary workstation computer.

So I wanted to write about this new iMac from the perspective of that entry-level iMac buyer, who chooses the iMac because it’s simple and then uses it until it breaks. What does a new iMac get you if you’re stepping up from the M1 model or comparing it against an older refurbished M4 model? Without any real external updates, what makes this iMac iteration interesting?

If you haven’t used an Apple Silicon-era iMac before, here’s a brief primer.

The new iMac is much thinner than the older aluminum Intel models. Its front is mostly one big 24-inch screen, surrounded on all sides by a relatively thick white bezel (I still wish black were at least an option, but Apple apparently doesn’t agree).

White bezel aside, it’s by far Apple’s most colorful computer. The iMacs ship with color-matched fronts, backs, stands, keyboards, mice, trackpads, and cables. Apple has tweaked the color offerings and changed the tints of each specific color a few times over the years, but the M6 iMac uses the same six color tints (plus silver) that the M4 iMac did.

The basic $1,499 iMac is essentially a MacBook Air in terms of ports—it has two Thunderbolt ports on the back and a headphone jack on the left side. Choosing this model also locks you into a lesser version of the Apple M6 with 10 CPU cores and 10 GPU cores instead of 12 of each, a maximum of 24GB of RAM, a minimum of 256GB of storage, and a maximum of 1TB. Its default keyboard doesn’t have Touch ID support, and the only way to get it is to buy the version with the built-in number pad, an $80 upsell.

The $1,699 version is the one to get if you still intend to use the iMac as a workstation. It includes the full-speed version of the M6 with all its CPU and GPU cores intact (though there are performance caveats here we’ll talk about later), plus options for 32GB of RAM and up to 2TB of storage. This version also includes four Thunderbolt ports on the back for connecting additional accessories. Both available keyboards on this version have Touch ID.

A 2.5 gigabit Ethernet port can be added to any iMac for $30. This port is located on the desktop’s power brick rather than the iMac itself, an elegant solution that keeps even a wired Internet connection hidden beneath your desk with the other cables. All iMacs also offer a VESA mount adapter option in place of the default stand at no extra charge.

I still really like the effect created by the color-matched keyboard, mouse/trackpad, and cables—even the white bezel complements the white keycaps and mouse. Just know you’re paying quite a lot for how the desktop looks. The $899 Mac mini is $800 cheaper than the iMac with the same chip, the same amount of RAM, and the same amount of storage. Sure, it would cost something to add a nice-looking 4K monitor, but not $800, even if you go for extra frills like a high refresh rate.

It’s fine that the iMac upcharges you for style and convenience. That’s the point of the computer. Just make sure that’s what you want to pay for.

Here are the basic specs for every entry-level iMac Apple has shipped, going all the way back to the last of the Intel models.

The iMac’s evolution has been slow and gradual during the Apple Silicon era. The computer has never gotten a base storage upgrade, and the base M4 and M6 models still only support a single external display (the upgraded models support two external displays, finally surpassing the old Intel model).

If you have any kind of Apple Silicon iMac and you’re happy with how it’s performing and you’re not hankering for more RAM or more displays, I don’t think the M6 model really adds anything worth upgrading for. It’s definitely faster, but even six years on, the Apple M1 remains perfectly serviceable for daily use, especially when it’s attached to 16GB of RAM.

And if you’re in the market for an iMac now, I think the M4 model still gets you the best bang for your buck if you can find one refurbished or on sale. That one still gets you a minimum of 16GB of RAM, Thunderbolt in all four ports for the four-port model, the 32GB RAM option, the nano-texture display option, and support for multiple external displays (again, in the four-port model).

The M6 iMac builds on that, and it comes with real performance benefits (more on that shortly). But the brightest dividing line in the Apple Silicon iMac model history is between the M3 and M4 model, not the M4 and M6 model. And that’s true despite the two-generation silicon upgrade.

A quick recap on the M6: a total of 12 CPU cores (two super cores, four performance cores, six efficiency cores), either 10 or 12 GPU cores, 16/24/32GB RAM options, and a doubled Neural Engine NPU with 32 cores instead of 16. For the iMac, the M6 also introduces some of the architectural tweaks introduced with the Apple M5, including the addition of neural accelerator hardware to the GPU cores. This boosts the speed of some kinds of local AI workloads compared to the M4, including MetalFX upscaling and frame generation in games.

The M6 in the Mac mini and the M6 in the 14-inch MacBook Pro both perform similarly within a roughly 28 W sustained power envelope. The version with 16GB of RAM will perform slightly worse in games and other workloads sensitive to memory bandwidth—the 16GB M6 offers 153 GB/s of memory bandwidth, and the 24GB and 32GB versions offer 170 GB/s. But that’s a pretty minor difference.

The iMac is slower than either of those two Macs, particularly in multi-core CPU benchmarks and graphics benchmarks, and the HandBrake video encoding test shows the likely culprit. The iMac’s M6 consumes an average of 18 W of power under sustained heavy load. It won’t throttle its speeds as aggressively as a MacBook Air because the iMac still includes an active cooling fan, but the reduced power envelope does mean that the Mac mini or MacBook Pro are 5 to 8 percent faster than the iMac in heavy multi-core CPU tasks and about 8 to 12 percent faster in graphics benchmarks.

One benefit of the smaller power envelope is that the iMac’s chip is even more power-efficient than the regular M6 and the iMac’s version of the M4, using much less power to do the same amount of work.

That still makes it a solid upgrade over the M4. The iMac’s M6 is 15 to 20 percent faster in our single-core CPU benchmarks, between 20 and 30 percent faster in multi-core CPU benchmarks, and between 20 and 40 percent faster in graphics benchmarks (the Blender GPU benchmarks do even better—they seem to like the neural accelerators in the M5 and M6 GPUs). Especially with 24GB or 32GB of RAM, it’s perfectly capable of being the heart of a multi-monitor workstation setup or a light photo/video editing rig.

The M6 iMac changes almost nothing about who the iMac is for, its position in Apple’s lineup, or what it’s technically capable of. It’s a box-ticking upgrade, and the box being ticked is “keep the insides up to date.”

That means today’s iMac is best for the same kinds of computer users it was good for in 1998: people who don’t want a laptop, people who need a friendly and approachable kiosk or office computer, and people who just want something pretty that doesn’t require a lot of fussing with screens or cables.

That’s the main thing I miss about being an iMac user. I like how my current Mac Studio desktop works, and I need flexibility and complexity in my specific setup to get the Mac and a gaming PC connected to all the same stuff. But despite my best efforts, the cable situation has always been a barely managed mess.

If someone you know has a cable management problem and doesn’t need the complexity, congratulations, you’ve found someone the iMac is for. I just wish Apple would use these kinds of pretty, saturated colors in a laptop or desktop that meets my needs, you know?

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

How Arsenal have evolved in 2026-27

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

There was a lot of speculation as to how Arsenal might change this season having flung a 22-year-old monkey (how long do monkeys live for?!) from their back in May by finally winning a league title. Would lifting the Premier League trophy lead to a new era of more expressive, urbane football?

I always had my doubts, not least because your style of play is always defined by your talent. Most of Arsenal’s elite talent is behind the ball. Arteta didn’t build Arsenal from the defence first, in my view, because he felt specifically that was the best way. It was just the area of the team that required the most attention when he was appointed.

In attacking areas, he had talent, even if it wasn’t balanced. Aubameyang, Lacazette and Pepe with Saka and Martinelli coming to the boil was a good collection of talent, even if the pieces didn’t always fit neatly together. The list of defenders he inherited included Mustafi, Sokratis, Holding, Chambers, Kolasinac and Mari. That’s why he went defence first, broadly speaking.

The moves the club have tried to make in recent seasons, especially this summer, shows you that Arteta wants his attack to be sharper than it is. For reasons I won’t relitigate here, the club were not able to add a number of high end attacking targets. Which means once again, Arsenal are reliant on Arteta’s coaching talents to squeeze more juice from the orange. Here are three things that have jumped out at me this season.

Arsenal spent a lot of money on Bruno Guimaraes this season and I will come onto some of the reasons why in the next section. But that signing has given Arteta a deep well of central options, with Rice, Bruno, Lewis Skelly, Odegaard, Merino and Zubimendi part of this midfield menagerie.

Arsenal use these options interchangeably and often according to game state. Art de Roche of the Athletic went into how Arteta has used the divergent profiles of Myles Lewis Skelly and Bruno Guimaraes so far in this campaign. In home games against Leeds and Everton, Lewis Skelly got the nod from the start.

In away games at Nottingham Forest and Liverpool, Bruno’s more robust approach was favoured. The pair have made significant impacts from the bench too. When the 1-0 victory at Anfield had become a little too ‘basketball’, Lewis Skelly came on and provided calm assurance in possession.

When the Gunners needed a little more belligerence away at Sunderland, Bruno came on and larruped the ball into the top corner from 25 yards. However, Arteta didn’t part with a significant part of his summer budget for a 28-year-old (he turns 29 next week but, for now, he remains 28 because that’s how it works) to be a high level rotation option.

Against Hull City last weekend, Bruno was selected and he played deeper than Declan Rice and Martin Odegaard. As Adrian Clarke pointed out in his match analysis piece, Bruno passed the ball to Odegaard 25 times in that match and the pair exchanged 35 passes in total. The idea was clear, to use Bruno’s punchiness in possession a little lower to feed Odegaard.

Against Everton, Mikel Merino was selected in midfield and Lewis Skelly started at left-back. I think this was about more than mere rotation. Arteta always highly respects the aerial capabilities of David Moyes’ Everton team, which makes sense since he played in a David Moyes Everton team for such a long time.

In 2023-24, Arteta selected Fabio Vieira ahead of Kai Havertz in midfield at Goodison Park because he wanted to keep the ball on the floor (Arsenal won that game 1-0 via a short corner routine because they did not see the logic of aerial corners against a team like Everton).

Last season at the Hill Dickinson Arsenal enjoyed a season high for passes attempted and completed because they wanted to keep the ball on the floor and avoid transitions. Arteta has a carousel available to him in midfield and a different club in the bag depending on what is called for on the day.

Per Opta, Arsenal have been caught offside 35 times so far this season, at least 11 more times than any other team. That rate of 3.5 offsides per game is the highest of any Premier League team since 2009. Kai Havertz has been flagged 15 times now, the next highest offender in the league is Dominic Calvert-Lewin with eight. Opta provide a deeper dive on those numbers here.

So what does this tell us, aside from the fact that Havertz needs to work on his timing? It means that Arsenal are far more willing to go in behind and they are willing to test the opposition backline earlier in moves. This was a big part of the reason they signed Bruno in the first place, Arteta wants to play with the risk reward dial with the ball.

Havertz also hasn’t been quite good enough to finish some of those one v one situations when he has got in behind the opponent’s back line. He fluffed his lines against Hull last weekend and in the 3-0 defeat at Brighton, he blasted his effort straight at the Brighton keeper when put through one v one.

But here, I will express my usual caveat, we need to normalise strikers missing chances and focus more on process than outcome. Most chances are missed, you want to worry when your striker is getting few shots. There have also been nascent signs that Arsenal have been using Gyokeres well in some of these situations.

That Havertz chance at the Amex was created by Gyokeres taking one touch with his back to goal and hooking the ball past the committed defensive line. Gyokeres did much the same for Madueke in Prague. It seems like Arsenal have worked on getting Gyokeres to use his physicality to initially retain the ball- something he badly struggled with last season- but creating situations where he can release it quickly.

Arsenal’s out of possession prowess is one thing that has been a constant from last season. It is Arsenal’s superpower, so even when they can only manage one goal, it is usually enough to take maximum points. To retain their superpower, Arsenal changed to stay the same, if that makes sense.

The signing of Ezri Konsa from Aston Villa was questioned by many but his presence has made the absence of a world class centre-back like William Saliba a sidebar, which is quite a feat. Arsenal ostensibly took William Saliba’s pizza chart and looked for the most Saliba like defender they could find and bought Konsa.

The loss of Saliba ought to have offered an enormous threat to Arsenal’s defensive stability. Arsenal’s tactical organisation out of possession is elite but so too are the individuals that helm it. Raya, Saliba, Gabriel and Rice are among the best players in the world in their respective positions.

Taking out one of those pillars ought to have derailed Arsenal’s defensive stability and it hasn’t because Konsa has the quality and poise to retain the team’s biggest strength. His pass completion stats are Saliba-esque and the quality of not giving the ball away needlessly is a significant part of the Gunners’ meanness.

How much Arsenal could improve in attack without significant additions was an unknown so Arteta could not afford to sacrifice the team’s defensive ceiling while he plays with those levers. The addition of Konsa was a personnel change that allowed Arsenal to remain the same.

British Airways Unveils 8 New Boeing 787 Heathrow Routes: See Full List Now

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Robin Guess | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

British Airways is one of only a tiny number of operators to fly all three variants of the Boeing 787. Ch-aviation shows that the oneworld member has 44 Dreamliners, which is the Boeing moniker. It has 18 787-9s (215 and 216 seats), 14 787-10s (256 seats), and 12 787-8s (204 seats).

The subfleet is entirely deployed from London Heathrow. However, BA eventually plans to replace its so-called Gatwick-configured 777-200ERs at the UK's second-busiest airport, which have fewer premium seats and higher overall capacity, with the 787-10. In time, Gatwick's widebodies will be all-Dreamliner.

OAG data was used to examine BA's 787 network from Heathrow. This looked at the routes available between January and September 2026, and then compared that to what's scheduled to operate from October 2026 through next year. This reflects what is known as of November 12 and certainly may change. Recent analysis shows that BA has pulled the 787 from six routes.

Up to five weekly (route switched from Gatwick, on which the 777-200ER was deployed)

Up to daily (route switched from Gatwick, on which the 777-200ER was deployed)

Daily (replaces the 777-200ER; route currently suspended)

Some of the routes are highly intriguing. This includes service to San José, the capital of Costa Rica and an increasingly important gateway for higher-end nature tourism. While the long route to Central America was flown by BA from Gatwick for the past decade, it is the first time that Heathrow has had flights there. The switch will help achieve higher yields and, somewhat paradoxically, a greater focus on connecting traffic.

On October 28, 2026, BA reintroduced the 787 on the short hop from Heathrow to Madrid, which links two critical IAG hubs. BA has flown widebody aircraft to Barajas for a long time. Iberia has too, and continues to do so on the A330 and A350 (I have flown the A350 on this route). The primary reason is to provide additional belly hold freight capacity to South America.

BA deployed the 767-300ER to Madrid for years, followed by the 777-200ER and the 787. The A380 appeared for crew familiarization and training. The so-called Dreamliner was used to Spain between 2017 and 2022, so the return after four years was notable. The type more or less replaced the Triple Seven, although that type is still going to appear occasionally this winter. The 777-200ER will replace the 787 next summer.

The 787-8, 787-9, and 787-10 are all scheduled to operate this route. This might reflect the lack of aircraft availability and 'slotting in' variants based on their availability. It is one of only six routes in the current month to see all three variants, although it's usually the case that one of the variants has very few flights, but they do exist.

Could Your GLP-1s Make You Blind? Here’s What to Make of the FDA’s Latest Notice.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

For years, there have been rumblings and even research about a rare but serious condition that’s been linked to taking a GLP-1 medication. Now, the Food and Drug Administration is doing something about it.

The FDA just released a drug safety communication to warn patients and healthcare providers about the potential increased risk of developing non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION) when you take a GLP-1 medication. NAION is a rare condition that can lead to vision loss in one eye, and it can be permanent.

Again, this isn't the first time concerns have been raised about the risk of NAION. But it is the first time the FDA has issued a warning about it.

Given that up to 11 percent of Americans currently take a GLP-1 for weight loss, this news stands to impact a lot of people. How concerned should you be if you're one of them? Eye experts and a doctor who prescribes GLP-1s break it down.

Meet the experts: Joseph Rizzo, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear, a member of the Mass General Brigham healthcare system; Bavand Youssefzadeh, DO, an ophthalmologist with Cedars-Sinai in Los Angeles and a cofounder of the Global LASIK and Cataract Institute; and Mir Ali, MD, the medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, California.

The FDA's notice is designed to flag the potential risk of NAION to people who use GLP-1s. The FDA also notes that the agency will require all manufacturers of GLP-1 medications to add information about the risk of NAION to the Warnings and Precautions section of their labels.

"Patients and caregivers should be aware of a potential increased risk of NAION, a rare condition that can result in acute, painless, decreased vision in one eye, including permanent vision loss, with GLP-1 RA use," the notice reads.

The agency cited a 2024 study published in JAMA Ophthalmology as well as its own meta-analyses and retrospective cohort study in the notice. They also noted that “NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 RA products.”

However, the complication remains rare: incidents from the FDA’s meta-analysis were just 6.4 events per 100,000 per year for patients using the medications.

The warning is just that—a warning to patients and physicians that this could happen, explains Joseph Rizzo, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear, a member of the Mass General Brigham healthcare system. "Most outcome-related issues in medicine should be thought of as being probabilistic—there is a certain chance that events can occur," he says. "This warning indicates that the FDA has reason to believe, at least at this time, that the probability of NAION occurring is higher in patients who take GLP-1 RA drugs."

But Dr. Rizzo stresses that there's no evidence that these drugs cause NAION—just that there's a link.

So…why issue a warning if it's not proven that GLP-1s cause NAION and it's so rare? "The severity of a complication matters alongside its frequency," says Bavand Youssefzadeh, DO, an ophthalmologist with Cedars-Sinai in Los Angeles and a cofounder of the Global LASIK and Cataract Institute. "Even a rare risk deserves attention when it can permanently affect vision." The warning is likely designed to give healthcare providers a heads-up that it could be NAION if a patient has symptoms, he explains.

"It's their duty to report any adverse effects, just so people are aware of them," says Mir Ali, MD, the medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, California.

While NAION is scary, doctors say it's important to put the data in perspective. "It's extremely rare, but it can happen," Dr. Ali says. Given how much GLP-1s can help with blood sugar management, weight loss, and downstream health effects, Dr. Ali says that the risk of NAION is "not a reason not to seek these medications."

Dr. Rizzo agrees. "I essentially never discourage the general use of GLP-1 RA drugs," he says. "Ultimately, the risk-to-benefit assessment must be decided by the patient, but physicians should provide sufficient information to the patients to better inform their decisions."

People should stay aware of the risk without panicking, Dr. Youssefzadeh says. "It's also worth remembering that the people most likely to be prescribed these drugs—people with diabetes, high blood pressure, sleep apnea—already carry a higher baseline risk for NAION, so the risk isn't starting from zero," he adds.

One More Test

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

As I get ever closer to finishing my hike, I must take on the northern Pennsylvania section notorious for its endless rocks.

A problem for future me. That is what I thought to myself as I finished my hike up and over, and then up and over again, Mount Moosilauke. I had finished the Whites, that meant the rest of the Appalachian Trail would be a cake walk. There was just one problem, northern Pennsylvania. Consistently rated the worst part of the AT not because of steep climbs, but because of the rocks. Throughout my time on the AT, I’d heard tales of this section, and some rather tall ones at that. I pushed the thought out of my mind. It would take me weeks to get there. Including New Hampshire, it was six states away. Well, now I am officially starting northern Pennsylvania. The problem for future me has become the problem for current me. To make matters worse, there was rain in the forecast overnight into tomorrow. It wasn’t just any old rocks I’d be contending with, it would be wet rocks. Lucky me.

I originally planned to drive across the Delaware River to the Dunnfield Creek Trailhead where I’d turned around yesterday. Since it was a lighter mileage day I decided to keep my van where it was and walk north to the trailhead, turn around and continue past the van southbound. This would add some miles to today, but trim them from tomorrow. It would also save a bit of toll expense since hikers aren’t charged for crossing the bridge. I made my way onto the bridge, briefly crossing back into New Jersey and along River Road. I was back at the van before 9:00AM and ready to tackle the climb up Mount Minsi.

On the bridge through the Delaware Water Gap.

Briefly back into New Jersey before leaving for the last time.

The trail passed the small Lenape Lake before ascending the south side of the Delaware Water Gap. Eureka Creek flowed across the Appalachian Trail which continued climbing up Mount Minsi until it reached a few rocky outcrops presenting hikers with a view high above the Delaware Water Gap. I had a perfectly clear view of Mount Tammany, with its rock slides corkscrewing upward. The trail turned along the ridge, relieving me of any more vertical steps. A dirt road made for easy hiking while passing by great views of the valley to the southeast. Soon the trail turned off the dirt road and I had my first encounter with the rocks of northern Pennsylvania. While small, they stretched on for far longer than any other rocky patch on the AT. I couldn’t hike at my usual pace, I would have twisted my ankle in no time. I paid extra close attention to navigating through the rocky maze, moving left and right to find the best spots to put my feet. The constant back and forth made it a chore to keep my momentum up. Still, it was more of an annoyance than a dreadful experience, at first.

The trail follows a gravel road briefly after Lenape Lake.

Looking across the Delaware Water Gap at Mount Tammany.

Following the ridge in northern Pennsylvania.

Crossing the road at Wolf Gap took a bit of courage as there is no line of sight down either side of the crest. I waited for the passing cars to clear and then listened for any new traffic approaching before jogging across the roadway. I was now within striking distance of Wold Rocks. As is customary, I stayed on the AT over Wolf Rocks, even though there is a bypass in case the weather is rainy and windy. That might be the case tomorrow, but all I had today were blue skies. This was the first scramble of the day, which only lasted a minute or so. What followed was a sloped rocky ridge with views to the northwest. Honestly, if it weren’t for the reputation of this section of the trail, I wouldn’t have thought the rocks were that bad, and it made sense for the trail to come up here for the view. I am sure more challenging terrain lay ahead.

Sure enough, this was about where things got more trying. The trail became something of a dense field of jagged rocks sticking up from the earth. The trail went on like this for mile after mile with only brief reprieves to be found. I was managing, but I cringed when thinking of what it would be like tomorrow in wet conditions. I fought my way to Wind Gap, a gap in the ridge that was thankfully not all that windy today. I checked in on a few more viewpoints before continuing the trek about 2 miles past the Leroy A. Smith shelter. Knowing there was finite daylight remaining I turned around to investigate the shelter.

Following the ridge in northern Pennsylvania.

I was struggling to decide where to spend the night. I was going to reach the Leroy A. Smith shelter with some daylight left. If the shelter was empty it might be a good idea to stay in it. I’d avoid a wet tent, but I’d have a longer hike back to the Delaware water gap. I also have had a lingering feeling ever since I passed on staying at the secret shelter that aid be able to get a much earlier start heading out from a shelter than my tent. Packing up camp requires light and warmth. It’s possible to pack up with just my headlamp, but there are a lot of things that could be accidentally left behind like tent stakes. It would be much easier with everything in plain sight in a shelter. If the goal was to finish hiking as early as possible, it might make more sense to stay in the shelter rather than hike a few miles up the trail and camp.

In the end, the decision was made for me. Someone had set up in the shelter already. Two dog bowls suggested that they either had a dog or unconventional eating habits. I dropped my backpack at the shelter and headed down to the spring. I encountered a man and a young dog at the spring. Both seemed friendly enough. The dog reminded me a lot of the dogs I used to foster when I lived in Maryland. It looked tough on the outside, but after a few good ear scratches it melted into a pup as gentle as they come.

I drank a liter of water at the spring and collected two more to get me through the morning. The pipe spring gushed water like a champ. It was quite a hike up from the spring. I noticed that water is rarely on the trail in this section because the mountain is so narrow. I’ll need to expect fewer streams right in front of shelters and more steep treks for water over the next week or so.

View from the trail near the Leroy A. Smith Shelter.

Back on the trail I had limited time to find a camping spot before light ran out. It seemed cruel that now that I was finally back in a state that allows camping along the trail with few restrictions, the areas near the trail rarely seemed fit for tenting. The trail was rocky, and so was all the flat ground near the trail. I was only sure of one spot about 2.5 miles back, I wasn’t going to make it there unless I wanted to retrieve my headlamp from my pack, which I was starting to consider. Before I could, I spotted what logged to be a suitable spot. I turned off the trail and proceeded to walk over the area, checking for sticks and rocks. It felt decent, so I rolled out my tent and felt the ground with my hands to see if there would be any lumps where my tent floor would be. I removed a few more rocks and sticks but was soon satisfied that the spot would work. I finished pitching the tent while my water filtered in the background. It wasn’t easy to get the stakes into the ground. A couple were more stuck between rocks than buried in the ground, but they seemed like they would hold. I went to bed hoping that the precipitation superintendent would cancel the rain tomorrow.

Still rocky, going to be hard to find a spot before it gets dark…

Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.
