Deadline looms for post-9/11 FirstNet reauthorization

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Supporters of the FirstNet program that provides emergency communications for first-responders still see hope for reauthorization this year, despite continued disagreements over what changes, if any, should be made to how the program is governed.

A House-passed reauthorization bill would shift authority toward the Commerce Department’s National Telecommunications and Information Administration and away from the FirstNet board, which is made up in part by first-responders.

The potential change is opposed by the influential Fraternal Order of Police, but any new bill or amendment would require floor votes in both the House and Senate, a potentially tall order as the 119th Congress draws to a close.

Authority for FirstNet, a program developed in the wake of 9/11, is set to expire in February of 2027 unless lawmakers pass an extension.

The program’s governing entity, the First Responder Network Authority, was created in 2012 in response to recommendations after the terrorist attacks, during which public safety agencies using different radio systems and overloaded phone lines struggled to adequately communicate.

The House passed its bipartisan reauthorization bill in April via voice vote. Senators have not yet taken up that legislation or introduced their own. In January, the Senate Commerce Committee’s Telecommunications and Media Subcommittee held a hearing on the program.

Related: First-responder network reauthorization advances

Craig Wheeler, a spokesman for subcommittee Chair Deb Fischer, R-Neb., said in a statement this week that reauthorization is still a priority.

“Sen. Fischer has been working closely with Chair Cruz on a bill and is having bipartisan discussions with Ranking Member Cantwell,” Wheeler said, referring to Sens. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, and Maria Cantwell, D-Wash.

“Moving a bill as soon as possible remains a top priority for Sen. Fischer, including getting it through both chambers and signed into law by President Trump.”

The House bill would extend authority for FirstNet through fiscal 2037, stagger terms on the authority’s board and require five members of the board with public safety backgrounds, rather than three as needed under current law.

At issue in the reauthorization debate, in part, is control of the First Responder Network Authority.

The House bill would eliminate language in current law that established FirstNet as an “independent” authority within the Commerce Department’s National Telecommunications and Information Administration and would require the administration’s approval for most non-planning or emergency actions, though the bill was amended to include a list of actions the network can take without NTIA approval.

The changes are meant to respond to reports from the Commerce Department’s Office of Inspector General that found that the AT&T-run service was not sufficiently available during the Maui wildfires in 2023 and that senior officials of the FirstNet Authority attempted to interfere with audits.

Related: First-responders network faced with calls for change

But the shifting of control drew opposition from the Fraternal Order of Police, which prior to the bill’s committee markup urged its members to instead support a “clean reauthorization” of the program.

Jim Pasco, executive director of the FOP, emphasized his group’s desire to see decisions made by the FirstNet board, rather than by NTIA. He said that moving authority away from a group that is required to include first-responders would be “a threat to officer and public safety.”

Yucel Ors, legislative director for public safety and crime prevention at the National League of Cities, said that his group, along with the International Association of Fire Chiefs and other state and local government and public safety groups, worked closely with the House, including on the changes to put some “everyday controls and functions” back directly under the board’s control.

He said the National League of Cities supported the House bill, but that they hope that any changes made to FirstNet’s governance are “not going to impact the day-to-day operations that public safety depend on, rely on, and expect.”

Ors said he expects the Senate would make changes to the House-passed bill, but he doesn’t know if those changes would come in the form of an amendment or an entirely new bill.

No proposed Senate amendment to the House bill, or alternative Senate bill, has yet to emerge.

He expressed concern over the timeline for reauthorization, especially if the Senate doesn’t take up the House bill as is.

A new Senate bill or amendment would require votes in both the House and Senate, with floor time dwindling as both chambers work through must-pass legislation before the end of the year.

Congress could potentially pass a short-term reauthorization of FirstNet. Ors expressed worry that option could jeopardize the public-private relationship with contractor AT&T.

Pasco said he would prefer a short reauthorization over the House-passed bill, but said he hopes a full reauthorization could still pass this Congress.

The House bill has strong bipartisan support and the Senate’s negotiations are also bipartisan. Both Ors and Pasco expressed confidence that if time runs out this year for a reauthorization bill, a new version of the legislation would still have strong prospects under a Democratic Congress.

American Cancer Society revises guidelines, urges routine PSA screening for prostate cancer

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Annalisa (Nalis) Merelli is focused on boys’ and men’s health: She looks into reproductive health, trending treatments, screening approaches, mental health, and everything in between.

In a major change to screening guidelines, the American Cancer Society recommended on Thursday that American men between ages 50 and 69 at an average risk of prostate cancer should test their prostate-specific antigen (PSA) blood levels every two years.

The prior guidelines, released in 2010, left testing for average-risk men to their discretion, after assessing cancer risk with a physician.

Similarly, PSA testing recommendations were strengthened for high risk groups — Black men and men with a strong family history of prostate cancer. They are now encouraged to get PSA-based screening yearly starting at 45. Black men in particular disproportionately die from prostate cancer, and the guideline authors said they hope that increased surveillance will reduce the number of Black patients presenting with cancers at advanced stages.

Then, starting at 70, the decision on whether to continue screening becomes individualized as the balance of benefit and harm is not as clear after that age. Screening should be interrupted after 80, or in men who are not expected to live another 10 years.

The changes reflect recent findings that screening through PSA levels reduced disease specific mortality for certain men, bringing the indicator back into specialists’ good graces. Once a preferred biomarker to identify risk of prostate cancer, routine PSA screening had fallen out of favor — and was dropped from the guidelines in 2010 — for its lack of specificity, which resulted in many men undergoing follow-up procedures when they had no cancer, or very slow-growing tumors.

The U.S. Preventive Service Task Force, a federally appointed panel of outside experts, has not changed its guidelines since the new evidence became available in 2025, and continues to recommend against routine, population-wide PSA screening, suggesting instead individualized decision-making. But the panel, whose recommendations determine the preventive services that insurers must cover, has announced an upcoming update.

The cutoff for PSA levels to be considered high is established as 3.0 nanograms/milliliter or higher for men aged 45 to 69 years old. This is an important clarification, said James Eastham, chief of urology at Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center. “Most PSA blood tests in the United States, when they’re reported, say under 4.0 [ng/ml] is normal, but it really should be under 3,” he said. “I think [the update] will hopefully help primary care physicians refer folks a little bit earlier.”

To limit the risk of overdiagnosis and overtreatment, the guidelines also now recommend two elevated PSA readings, followed by an MRI scan and/or testing for other biomarkers of prostate cancer, with the aim of reducing the number of patients undergoing biopsies. “We just wanted to give a little bit of flexibility for folks who cannot easily obtain an MRI,” said William Dahut, chief scientific officer at the ACS and a contributing author of the guidelines, about including both biomarkers and MRIs as a possible step following elevated PSA levels. However, he noted that an MRI would still be recommended prior to biopsy, should a biomarker test come back abnormal.

Though there are a variety of biomarkers, the guidelines don’t recommend one over the other. Dahut said ACS researchers  intend to look more closely at biomarkers to establish which may be more effective in predicting a more aggressive cancer.

“I think the new guidelines are certainly a balanced approach for early detection of prostate cancer,” said Eastham. “There have been many men undergoing unnecessary biopsies, and many low risk cancers that were subsequently overtreated,” he said. By recommending some steps between an elevated PSA and a biopsy, he said, the new recommendations reflect the evidence that while being a good biomarker, PSA alone would identify too many men as biopsy candidates.

Finally, the guidelines emphasize the importance of active surveillance in men with slow growing cancer: “If you screen more men, you’re going to find more men with early-stage disease, and we need to be strong in our position that that does not mean that you need to rush for therapies or that you need to have therapies at all.”

For Eddie Baynes, a 56-year-old prostate cancer survivor and advocate, the new guidelines “hit the nail on the head” when it comes to recommending routine PSA screening. As a Black man, he knew he was at higher risk of developing prostate cancer, yet it wasn’t until the fraternity he belonged to organized a screening event that he, at 52, started getting regular PSA tests with his bloodwork. Though routine screening wasn’t part of the ACS recommendation at the time, it led to a cancer diagnosis and a prostatectomy.

That’s why he’s been advocating for men to get PSA levels tested, and relatively frequently. With the new guidelines, men like him will start routine screening earlier than he did. “Part of my advocacy is getting tested more frequently. So now, just to have that announcement coming from the American Cancer Society, it just makes what I’ve been urging individuals to do for a while stronger,” he said.

STAT’s coverage of health challenges facing men and boys is supported by Rise Together, a donor advised fund sponsored and administered by National Philanthropic Trust and established by Richard Reeves, founding president of the American Institute for Boys and Men; and by the Boston Foundation. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Annalisa (Nalis) Merelli is focused on boys’ and men’s health: She looks into reproductive health, trending treatments, screening approaches, mental health, and everything in between.

New Witcher 3 DLC Uses AI to Touch Up Geralt's Polish Voice Due to Actor's Health Issues

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

CD Projekt Red has worked with an AI voice generation company on next year's The Witcher 3 expansion, Songs of the Past, so the Polish voiceover for Geralt can match the original game.

Jacek Rozenek, the original Polish voice actor for Geralt has returned to voice the character for all the content in Songs of the Past. However, the actor unfortunately suffered a stroke in recent years that impacted his voice. This has left his voice unrecognisable compared to the lines he recorded for the original RPG.

Having received Rozenek's blessing, CD Projekt Red recorded all his voice work for Songs of the Past, and has taken that work to AI company Respeecher, who will make it sound like Geralt from the base game.

Again, this only affects the Polish language option. Doug Cockle has returned to voice Geralt for the English language version.

Rozenek suffered a stroke in 2019, and he was unable to speak following it. Through hours of training every day, he was able to return to voice acting in 2022, when CD Projekt Red released the PS5 version of The Witcher 3. There was a new quest in that edition, and the work he had to put into the voice lines accompanying it left Rozenek "exhausted" after the recording.

Rozenek has recorded his Polish voice lines for Songs of the Past as usual, but he's supportive of the developer's decision to involve Respeecher to make them sound like Geralt: "The point is that this voice should be as close as possible to my voice from 12 years ago. I have great trust... for the creators of this game."

Respeecher has used AI tools trained on Rozenek's previous work as Geralt to match the voices as much as possible.

News Here's 13 Minutes of Witcher 3: Songs of the Past DLC Gameplay

"We basically delivered to Respeecher all the lines that Jacek recorded for us for The Witcher 3,” explained localisation producer Mikołaj Szwed. “They created an algorithm of his voice, based on his lines."

Szwed added that CD Projekt Red only considered this route because of Rozenek's health issues, and having gained his blessing, thinks this is an "ethical" use of AI that can do some good.

Songs of the Past is slated to launch for PS5 at some point in 2027, off the back of the recently released The Witcher 3 Remastered.

Amid soaring PC hardware prices, gaming monitors are getting cheaper and cheaper. Here's why, and what to buy

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Hello, good local-time-zone-adjusted afternoon, and let's just say it's been a while. But I come back to you now at the turn of the technological tide. Well, sort of. Sadly I'm not here to predict an end to the ridiculous AI-fuelled chip crisis. That whole mess has a couple of years to run, surely.

Instead I speak of screens, where there is actually some very good news. In short, have you seen how cheap PC gaming monitors are? I mean remarkably, almost stupendously, cheap. I'm talking £60 / $60 for an entry-level 1080p panel with triple-digit refresh rate, sub-£200 / $250 for high-refresh 4K gaming. Heck, even OLED monitors now start with a "2" in both currencies.

Also bear in mind we've had a fair old bit of inflation in recent years. A 4K panel bought today for £200 is equivalent to paying roughly £150 back in 2020. All the while, that same £200 / $200 right now won't buy you a current-model gaming graphics card at all, even the most feeble example, and will barely secure a basic dual-channel 16GB DDR5 memory kit. Youch.

So, I think we can all agree monitors are cheap in the current chip-catastrophe context. The immediate question is why? The answer isn't all that complicated, actually. Most obviously, the display market isn't being devoured by the AI craze. All those data centres are stuffed with GPUs, memory chips and NAND flash, not monitors.

By way of example, one of the largest new AI data centres on Earth is Microsoft's new Fairwater site in Mount Pleasant, Wisconsin. It's thought to pack hundreds of thousands of Nvidia Blackwell GPUs. The cost doesn't bear thinking about, but those GPUs are built into what's known as "headless" server racks. In other words, they have no screens at all. Ultimately, the key compute hardware is all managed remotely, in which capacity the Fairwater facility is reported to currently have around 375 employees. If we generously assume each has two PC monitors, that's only 750 for the entire site.

AI isn't really driving demand on the end-user side of the equation, either. Put it this way: you don't need to buy a new phone, laptop or PC to access the latest models. At the same time, the display market is suffering from something of a glut of overcapacity. Chinese companies built out huge capacity in LCD panel manufacturing over the past decade, to the extent factories are now running underutilised. Meanwhile, even the OLED market is arguably oversupplied.

In fact, Samsung and LG, the two big noises in large OLED panel manufacturing (as opposed to OLED displays for tablets and phones), have been shifting emphasis away from TV panels to PC monitors because the latter offer better margins. Meanwhile, the AI boom has almost definitely meant lower demand for new PCs. Certainly, the big research houses point to cratering PC sales thanks to higher component costs. Long story short, that's oodles of capacity combined with weakening demand. Like I said, the explanation for cheap monitors isn't exactly rocket surgery, if you know what I mean.

What is a bit more complicated is making sense of what's available today in the gaming monitor market. With so much choice, where exactly is the very best value to be found? And are there any remaining pain points?

Monitor specifications, to be sure, can be tricksy at the best of times. But if you're looking for some blanket rules to fling casually over the whole LCD monitor market - I'll be digging deeper into OLED tech another day - try these for size. Look for, at minimum, 300 nits brightness and 1ms response times. Refresh rates are a slightly more complicated call, but for the vast majority of games, 144Hz is plenty. For "esports-grade multiplayer fare like Counter-Strike or Dota 2, or I think a refresh in the high 200s will get the job done for most. If you'll benefit from more than that, you almost certainly know who you are already.

Oh, and heavily favour IPS over VA panels. Exceptions exist: there are some decent "Rapid VA" panels these days and Samsung, in particular, does some nippy VA models. But a lot of the cheaper stuff is pretty slow and smeary, making VA a risky buy. Finally, don't worry too much about claimed HDR capability, save for the fact that HDR 400 certification at least means the monitor can do 400 nits. Unless a monitor has local dimming, it won't be truly HDR capable.

Anywho, one immediate and rather ironic issue is the growing mismatch between monitors and GPUs. For sure, it's great you can grab a high-refresh 4K monitor for such little money. It's almost hard to believe that you can have this 27-inch IPS MSI for sub-£200 (you're looking at $250 for the same model in the US), and it even has a 288Hz 1080p dual mode.

I actually favour 32 inches with my 4K gaming, and that's naturally a little pricier, especially if you insist on an IPS - which again, you probably should. But even $350 for that kind of panel (here's a comparable model for £340 in the UK) is a fraction of what you'd have paid for a similarly specced model a few years ago. And it probably wouldn't have included dual-mode functionality.

Here's the problem, though. How are you going to drive the thing, what with its eight million-plus pixels? After all, the cheapest RTX 5090 on Newegg right now is over $6,500. So high-end Nvidia GPUs now cost about as much as a decent used car.

Of course, you might be surprised just how effective the likes of an AMD RX 9070 XT or Nvidia RTX 5070-class GPU is at 4K thanks to upscaling technologies including DLSS and FSR. The latest transformer model upscalers really do knock out pretty stellar image quality. Add in a bit of frame gen and you have a compelling combination of visual sharpness, reasonably low latency and nice smoothframe rates, even in the most demanding of games. Perhaps the one thing you can't do is enable full path tracing in a game like Cyberpunk 2077 and still expect a slick experience, but standard ray tracing remains an option.

The bigger snag, unsurprisingly, is that you can no longer bag a 5070 at (let alone below) its original £539 / $549 MSRP. Instead, you're looking at the thick end of £800 / $900. That's a bit of an ask for pairing with a £200 monitor, isn't it?

I'd therefore argue that, for pure gaming purposes, the bang-for-buck sweet spot remains just where it's been sat twiddling its thumbs for the better part of a decade, namely 1440p. That applies to both its vanilla 16:9 aspect variety and my personally preferred ultrawide format, the latter being so very immersive and also pretty nifty for daily multi-tasking. Indeed, the 27-inch 1440p segment even brings the vibrant colours and infinite contrast of OLED panels into a reasonably affordable window.

By way of example, £179 (or $150) is enough for a circa 200Hz, IPS 1440p panel with super low (by IPS standards) pixel response, even if the 250-nit brightness is lower than I'd like. Meanwhile, as I write these words, pricing for big-brand 27-inch 1440p gaming OLEDs kicks off at about £290 in the UK and roughly $270 in the US. Admittedly, those entry-level OLEDs come with some compromises, which I'll cover in a forthcoming post. But what you might call 'proper' OLED panels can be had around the low £300 / $300 mark - not throw-away money, for sure, but relatively accessible.

The 34-inch 1440p ultrawide segment is a bit trickier, however. The good news is that conventional LCD models can be had for relative peanuts, with prices starting at about £220 or $249 for game-focused, high-refresh models. But those two example both use a VA panel, which usually means inferior pixel response.

IPS models are a little thin on the ground when it comes to 34-inch ultrawide, but sneaking in below £300 / $300 is generally possible. The real bummer is OLED, where going ultrawide invokes a massive premium. The best price I can find on a big-brand 34-inch ultrawide today is $629 for this Asus model in the US and £648 for this MSI option in the UK.

That's awfully toppy, especially when you consider that you can grab a 49-inch, 5,120x1,440 ultrawide OLED for barely any more money. I do actually quite like those 49-inch ultrawides, but I have to concede the ergonomics are challenging. However, 4K OLEDs in 27-inch and 32-inch formats can also be had for as much as £100 / $100 less than the current cheapest 34-inch 1440p ultrawides, which doesn't make much sense to me.

Again, with 4K, there's the challenge of keeping all those pixels updated. If you already have a sufficiently mighty graphics card, though, I a 32-inch 4K OLED can make a fantastic all-round option, and something like this MSI model for £549 would be absolutely lovely. You get good pixel density and lots of space for daily desktopping, plus OLED visuals and a large enough panel to be really get you into your games.

It's just a pity that 34-inch OLED ultrawides remain so weirdly expensive, it being a format that ticks so many different boxes. As for my own personal money-no-object pick, well, I'm typing these words on the new Alienware AW3926QW, the 39-inch 5K2K beast with a Tandem WOLED panel and RGB subpixels.

One could argue it's a relative steal at £1,079 or $1,100. I mean, it's pretty much peak current PC gaming display technology, and it's about half the price of what Nvidia's second-tier RTX 5080 graphics card is going for lately. Assuming you can even find one in stock.

At this point, the one major category I haven't covered is LCD monitors with mini-LED full-array local dimming. Cards on the table, I'm not a huge fan of mini-LED monitors. The detailed reasons for that are, perhaps, the subject for another day. But I've reviewed countless models and have yet to see one that didn't come with pretty serious compromises.

That said, the technology has gotten a lot more accessible in the last 12 to 18 months, to the extent that 27-inch 1440p models start at just over $250. I've had that specific model in and came away pretty impressed. It's very, very punchy, and even factoring in the limitations of mini-LED versus OLED when it comes to lighting control and speed, I cannot deny the temptation.

Just for completeness, one further exception to the general rule of cheap gaming monitors involves ultra-high refresh monitors aimed at serious esports aficionados. I'm talking about panels like this 1,000Hz LG I sampled elsewhere. At £800 / $1,000 for a 25-inch 1080p IPS panel it's inevitably a niche proposition, and poor value by normal standards.

I think a much better compromise for just about everyone, short of an actual esports pro, is to grab a larger, higher resolution monitor that offers one of those windowed 1080p modes running at about 500Hz or thereabouts. That way you've got a nice high-res panel for both work and play, as well as that ultra-low latency mode for when you want to dance around Dust 2 doling out the headshots or whatever.

Incidentally, I realise I've gone in a bit hot and heavy with the OLED content here, the implicit assumption being OLED is all that, and notably without addressing the obvious objections like the risk of burn-in. But all that, I hope you'll be pleased to hear, is very much the subject for my next RPS post on all things OLED.

Until then, all I can say is thank goodness PC monitors provide relative respite from the parlous state of much of the rest of the PC component market. It's absolutely brutal out there if you're building a gaming PC, but at least you'll get a lot of screen for your money.

Just to round things out, if anyone has any specific queries at any given price point, form factor or panel type, shout out below and I'll try to steer you in the direction of a decent model.

Rock Paper Shotgun is better when you sign in

Sign in and join us on our journey to discover strange and compelling PC games.

Victorian Psycho Review: Maika Monroe Goes Crazy In This Erratic Mix Of Humor And Horror

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Horror-comedies tend to lean heavier on the "comedy" side of things than the "horror." But Zachary Wigon's "Victorian Psycho" attempts to do the opposite, giving the audience a gaggle of gruesome, unsettling moments punctuated by wry humor. It makes for an erratic viewing experience — you begin to feel the film would be better served staying in one lane rather than trying to make concepts like dead babies funny. Regardless, "Victorian Psycho" mostly succeeds due to Maika Monroe, who gives everything she's got as Winifred Notty, a psychotic governess who moves in with a wealthy family and proceeds to pile up a body count.

Her attempt at an English accent may be a bit spotty, but Monroe's commitment to playing crazy ultimately works, and works well. Her wry narration guides us through outlandish, blood-drenched moments as she spins a twisted tale. If the film's title (an obvious nod to "American Psycho") wasn't a dead giveaway, it's clear from the jump that Winifred is ... not well. A suspicious past follows her as she arrives at Ensor House, inhabited by the Pounds family.

Winifred may be deranged, but the Poundses aren't exactly a pleasant bunch. Mr. Pounds (Jason Isaacs) is oblivious, Mrs. Pounds (Ruth Wilson) is cruel and ruthless with the staff, and the children Andrew (Jacobi Jupe) and Drusilla (Evie Templeton) have their own issues (Andrew is a brat destined to grow up to be a bully and Drusilla is more than a little creepy). A sane person would take one look at the Pounds family and head for the hills, but of course, Winifred isn't sane.

There's plenty of room for fun here, but "Victorian Psycho" is never confident enough in what it's trying to do. The performances are all very broad and big, save for the always-welcomed Thomasin McKenzie, playing the good-natured but clueless nurse Ms. Lamb, who becomes Winifred's only real friend (and instantly buys into her quickly concocted story about a ghoul roaming the grounds of Ensor House).

As big and over-the-top as many of the elements here are, "Victorian Psycho" also seems to be trying to make a point about the subjugation of women in polite society. Yet it has no real insight into this beyond a surface level approach, and it's hard to get caught up in any sort of message when the film is also constantly pointing out how ridiculous everything here is.

Wigon, who previously directed "Sanctuary," piles on some energetic camerawork that frequently mirrors Winifred's fractured mind, but the script by Virginia Feito, adapting her own novel, relies too much on incessant narration that's not nearly as funny as it should be. And yet, "Victorian Psycho" remains perpetually watchable primarily due to Monroe's performance. A certified scream queen at this point in her career, it's a lot of fun to see Monroe cut loose and swing an axe at people's heads.

"Victorian Psycho" is just a little too unstable for its own good. There's a specific reason why Winifred has shown up at Ensor House, but the slow reveal of what's going on here feels undercooked. And when the film reaches its grand guignol finale, Wigon inexplicably starts pulling punches, robbing "Victorian Psycho" of a more satisfying conclusion and instead attempting something far more serious and dramatic than everything we've seen so far.

What this all comes down to is me wanting to like "Victorian Psycho" a lot more than I ultimately did. I enjoyed Monroe's performance, and the film's morbidly funny atmosphere, but I came away from it all a tad unsatisfied. There's a ghoulish charm to the narrative, but the blood and guts of something far better is lurking somewhere in here, just out of sight.

"Victorian Psycho" opens in theaters on November 13, 2026.

Rolling Stone Essentials 2027

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

From fresh takes on familiar staples to unexpected game-changers and brands that simply have it, these are the companies and products earning our ultimate stamp of approval.

From fresh takes on familiar staples to unexpected game-changers and brands that simply have it, these are the companies and products earning our ultimate stamp of approval

It was a big year for smartphone releases, and amid all of the foldables, one of the most exciting innovations almost flew under the radar. Samsung’s display on the S26 Ultra has a built-in privacy setting that can even be toggled on for specific parts of the screen (such as a pop-up notification from the bank, for example). We got to see the display in action, and it’s as impressive as promised — the screen is legible and bright when looking directly at it but nearly invisible at an angle.

ASUS had a big year of releases, and while a lot of the attention went to the flashy dual-screen models the brand unveiled, their traditional laptop releases were worth paying attention to as well. The Zenbook A16 has a lightweight design without sacrificing screen size, and the dazzling OLED display also features a touchscreen. You get more ports than you typically expect on a modern laptop, including two USB-C ports, a USB-A port, an HDMI port, and an SD card reader. Overall, it’s a powerful laptop that suits a range of use cases.

The Pixel Watch does what few smartwatches have been able to pull off — look like an actual watch. With a domed display, the face doesn’t call attention to the fact that it’s a screen, and the latest Pixel Watch 5 packs in all of the features you need, including health tracking and sleep tracking, plus features you want but hopefully won’t need, like car-crash detection and SOS satellite communications.

Best known for their affordable TVs, TCL has been raising the bar to become one of the best all-around TV brands, with the right combination of innovation, quality, and value. Their SQD mini-LED TVs deliver greater brightness and contrast, with up to 3,000 peak nits for a vibrant display even in bright rooms.

Boox has proven that an e-reader can be more than an e-reader. The brand’s versatile e-ink gadgets run the gamut from phone-sized e-readers to large note-taking tablets. Because they run Android, you can download a versatile suite of apps. Their latest device is the itty-bitty Picco, which is roughly the size of a playing card. With support for ePub and a microSD slot, you can take a variety of file types to read on the go.

Gaming consoles are dominated by a few brands, but Nex has shaken up the industry with its family-friendly gaming console. The cube-shaped device features a movement-tracking camera, so each game encourages movement and activity. To protect privacy, the camera doesn’t save or store video or audio, and there’s an added physical camera cover for additional privacy. The Playground console also comes with five games to start.

Skylight bills itself as a family-oriented product, designed to help corral kids’ schedules and organize chores. But it’s surprisingly useful in child-free households, or even at the office. As editors who tend to find appointments and obligations sneaking up on us, the easy at-a-glance organization makes it easy to figure out what we have going on that day, and it syncs easily with a variety of calendar apps. The subscription unlocks more, but you can still do a lot with the free tier.

SwitchBot makes a dizzying array of smart home products, with wide support for a range of platforms. The smart locks let you use everything from fingerprints to facial recognition, while the cameras are a more affordable alternative to other tech brands. You can also build a whole-home ecosystem with accessories like smart window shades and lighting. With so much smart tech, it’s easy to forget SwitchBot started as just a Bluetooth button-pushing gizmo.

XGIMI makes projectors for every kind of enthusiast, from home theater pros to on-the-go gamers, and this year marked some major releases from the brand. There was the cinematic Titan Noir Max, the space-saving short-throw Aura 3 Max, and the ultra-portable battery-powered Vibe One. Across price and class, XGIMI has been delivering stylish design, excellent picture quality, and user-friendliness.

It’s rare to see a headphone brand actually shake up the big players, but Daisy proved to be one of the buzziest if-you-know-you-know brands thanks to its stellar design. The aluminum accents set it apart from the all-plastic construction of most headphones, but they look a lot more expensive than they are. Tactile controls make for a refined experience, while solid sound and noise-canceling hold up the performance end of the equation.

PSB’s iQ bookshelf speakers are some of the most stylishly contemporary speakers we’ve seen, with a unique design that puts the bass driver on top of the tweeter. The speakers also connect to each other wirelessly, adding greater flexibility in where you can plug them in. As for listening to music, they support BluOS for multiroom audio, and they have inputs for HDMI eARC, RCA, optical, USB-C, and USB-A, making them flexible and versatile.

Year after year, Q Acoustics proves to be a hi-fi brand that enthusiasts keep coming back to. Their latest release is the 3040C floorstanding speakers, which are headed to the U.S. market soon. They’re slimmer and sleeker than most tower speakers, but they don’t compromise on sound, with clear highs and distortion-free bass. Eye-catching yet unobtrusive, they’re also a stylish addition to any living room or home theater.

Nanu is all about taking a less-is-more approach. The brand currently offers two products, a mechanical counter and an alarm clock. We’ve been using the Arc alarm clock in our daily routine, and it might be the low-tech answer we’ve been waiting for. It has a sturdy metal build and a pleasing retro look, but the digital alarm adjustment is more precise than most analog alarm clocks. Yes, it’s pricey, but after accidentally breaking a series of cheap digital clocks (we’re not morning people), we’re glad to have something that feels built to last.

I’ve been lucky enough to test a lot of impressive audio products, but it’s rare to find one that truly blows me away. KEF’s R11 Meta fit the bill, delivering an exceptional amount of detail, even in rooms that are less than ideally set up.

As for sound, each speaker gets plenty loud thanks to the four bass drivers. There’s also an aluminum mid-range driver that houses a 1″ vented aluminum dome tweeter, which means the bass doesn’t overwhelm the mids or highs. Given their size and low impedance rating, you’ll want an appropriately powerful amplifier to power them.

They’re strikingly good-looking and are available in glossy black or white finishes, or more classic walnut. They are also, it should be noted, gigantic; getting them home and unboxing them was its own odyssey.

It won’t shock you that the R11 Meta speakers are an investment. They’re $3,500 (for one speaker; the pair will set you back $7,000), so I could hardly blame KEF for making sure the pair was returned promptly after I tried them. I might not be in the requisite tax bracket to justify buying these permanently, but they’ve earned a spot on my wishlist. —Jonathan Zavaleta

I’ve been lucky enough to test a lot of impressive audio products, but it’s rare to find one that truly blows me away. KEF’s R11 Meta fit the bill, delivering an exceptional amount of detail, even in rooms that are less than ideally set up.

I might not be in the requisite tax bracket to justify buying these permanently, but they’ve earned a spot on my wishlist. —Jonathan Zavaleta

Beyond Yoga takes activewear well past the workout. Sure, the brand has gear for just about every activity you can think of, but it also has seriously comfortable pieces you’ll want to change into after you finish your reps. Everything they make is top-notch, right up to the hats. And the best part? The price point gives the other guys some serious competition.

Cozy Earth might be famous for its beautiful bedding, but the comfort giant has another home essential on lock: loungewear. From ridiculously soft pajamas and matching sweat sets to slippers for the whole family, the brand has staying-in style down to an art. The lineup is so good, it might just give those bestselling sheets a run for their money.

What makes good denim? In our minds, the best jeans should wrap modern, comfortable design, high quality, and sustainability in a package that most consumers can afford. If you need an example of top-tier denim production, look to DL1961. The New York City label makes all of its denim in a family-owned factory using premium materials, and their considerate approach comes through in the final product. They’ve also got a great rotation of washes and fits, ensuring something for nearly every personal preference.

Buck Mason is arguably the biggest menswear success story of the past decade. Their pieces would look great in any era over the last seventy-odd years, and they feel like they’ll last into the next couple of decades at least. If we were building a menswear wardrobe, Buck Mason would be our first stop.

Spanx has just about every corner of the women’s wardrobe covered, from the bottom up. Its shapewear is a total game-changer, its undergarments offer support where it counts, and its clothing is as flattering as it is comfortable. From office-ready workwear to essentials for a night out, Spanx has everything you need to wear, both underneath it all and on the outside.

It takes a licking, keeps on ticking, and we can’t stop recommending it. Timex has been in peak form for the last several years, putting out achievement-inspired and collaborative pieces that are just plain cool. And their watches just happen to be very affordable, too. Their catalog is very deep, so almost everyone will find something they like, whether it’s the Nineties-inspired Intrepid x Dimepiece, the Bespoke Post x Timex Heritage 66 dive watch, or the slick Cavatina Mini dress watch.

There’s a mountain of outerwear out there, but not every brand knows how to balance comfort, style, and performance. Free Country does all three. Founded in the Nineties, the brand makes best-selling layers that can go as rugged or refined as you want, whether you’re tackling the outdoors or just sipping cocktails on a patio.

Swimwear is a tricky art: It needs to be stylish, flattering, comfortable, and built to perform. Vilebrequin checks every box. Born in Saint-Tropez, the brand brings bold prints, endless silhouettes, and seriously standout designs to the beach. It’s the kind of swimwear that will completely change how you feel about shopping for bathing suits in the first place.

Footwear is a tough space to crack, but Alohas has pulled it off. After a decade in business, the Barcelona-based label has established itself as one of the most reliable sources of high-quality shoes that are on-trend, sustainable, and reasonably priced. That’s a rare combination. Best of all, they produce footwear in categories from sneakers to dress shoes that all rival much pricier designer pairs.

Ana Luisa has mastered the art of having it all (at least when it comes to jewelry): Quality, innovation, and plenty of personality. It’s no surprise that one of the co-founders previously worked with some of the world’s best-known designer brands before taking what he learned and turning it into something special. From fun, customizable costume items to finer pieces worth gifting (and keeping for yourself), Ana Luisa makes jewelry shopping feel anything but ordinary.

There’s nothing quite like the thrill of finding a hidden gem at a thrift store, but when you don’t have a free Sunday to spend digging through Brooklyn racks, ThredUp brings the hunt straight to your couch. Shop hundreds of designers and beloved brands, or sell pieces from your own closet. It’s secondhand shopping without the noise.

How do you pack for a safari? Well, if you’re toting the Peak Design Travel Duffel, the answer is pretty simple. I lugged the lightweight carry-on to Africa this year, and it held up to everything from a sudden rainstorm in an open SUV to a sandstorm in the Serengeti. That’s thanks to Peak Design’s proprietary “Versa Shell” material, which is fully weatherproof and reinforced to withstand wear and tear (scrapes and scuffs were also easily wiped off with a damp cloth). The practical thinking extends to the duffel’s storage capabilities, with two internal mesh pockets for organization and four external zip pockets for easy access. Thoughtful details abound, like flaps over the zippers to prevent water from getting in, and a waterproof bottom liner that helps the bag sit flat for easy packing. A padded shoulder strap and two top handles offer multiple ways to carry the duffle, whether you’re running to make a connection or tossing the bag in a bush plane. Peak Design offers multiple colorways, but the all-black duffel is minimalist and modern-looking, offering a bag that’s as streamlined as your travels. —Tim Chan

There are hundreds of face washes out there, but Pour Tous gets our ultimate seal of approval for keeping things simple. No need to hunt down a specialty cleanser for your skin type or concerns: This one is made for everyday use, morning and night, with an affordable price tag that’s just as refreshing.

PROVEN is the Goldilocks of moisturizers: Not too heavy, not too light, but just right. Its creams glide on without feeling caked or leaving behind an overpowering scent, while helping keep dry, cracked skin at bay. From eye cream to night cream, the lineup has your hydration needs covered.

Think you can skip serum in your skincare routine? Think again. With so many formulas competing for your attention, TimeSavvy makes the decision a whole lot easier. Its dermatologist-developed makeup combines everyday skincare with sun protection, making it an easy addition to your “get ready with me” routine.

Vacation makes the kind of sunscreen you actually look forward to wearing. Of course, you should always take your SPF seriously, but the brand makes reapplying feel less like a chore and more like part of the fun. Between its incredible scents and seriously adorable packaging, even a New York sidewalk can feel like a tropical escape.

Blue Lagoon Skincare comes all the way from Iceland (yes, that Blue Lagoon), and its products make you feel like you’ve discovered a little piece of magic. The eye cream is a standout, helping visibly reduce the look of dark circles and puffiness. Sometimes the best beauty secrets come from the middle of the North Atlantic. Luckily, no passport is required.

SEEN proves that good haircare should do more than just look pretty on your bathroom shelf. Its products help make hair look and feel noticeably better, while its customizable bundles let you build a routine around exactly what your hair needs. No guesswork, no overcomplicated regimens — just really good haircare in seriously sexy packaging.

Most hairstyling brands are slick with no substance, but Forte Series eschews hype in favor of results. The grooming brand’s line of everyday products includes refreshing texture sprays, volumizing shampoos, gentle hair oils, and quick-fix styling aids that address everything from frizz to flatness. We like the molding paste, which offers a flexible hold, matte finish, and no sticky residue. From hair repair to hair care, Forte Series offers the best products to help you lock in your look.

The name might be French, but Nécessaire is an American-founded brand that has turned everyday self-care into something special. A cult favorite with celebrity appeal, its body washes leave skin feeling like you’ve just stepped out of a five-star spa. The lingering scents are just an added plus.

Salt & Stone tapped Role Model for a buzzy campaign in 2026, but the deodorant brand has long been a fan favorite thanks to its aluminum-free formula, minimalist packaging, and fresh and modern scents. Skin-nourishing ingredients keep your underarms soft and supple, and 48-hour protection means you’re covered, even if you skip a swipe. Whether you’re headed to a sweaty concert or under pressure at work, consider the brand an essential pit stop.

Gillette dared to go where few shaving brands have gone before with the launch of its GilletteLabs Body Razor, and dare we say – it makes the cut. The razor delivers a smooth and gentle glide, with evenly-spaced blades that won’t get caught in your hair (or worse). A grippy handle and flexible head allow you to reach even the most sensitive areas with ease, and it’s smooth sailing from there. Gillette has long been the first choice for our face, but as grooming routines get personal, it’s now the best razor brand for our bodies too.

Braun is making a compelling case to bring the electric shaver back, thanks to its NEVO 11000c. Made in Germany with stainless steel construction, the handheld trimmer is as durable as it is precise, with a smart processor designed to adapt in “real time” to your facial hair density. What you get: An ultra-smooth and comfortable shave, with a flexible head that hugs the dimples and crevices of your face without irritation. When you’re done, plug the shaver into the NEVO Care System dock, which automatically cleans and charges the blades. This is advanced shaving made simple.

Fragrance is deeply personal, but OneKind makes finding your next signature scent feel like the best kind of shopping spree. Its clean products offer plenty of options to suit different tastes and vibes, making it nearly impossible to settle on just one.

Medicube may have serious celeb backing (see everyone from Kylie Jenner to Lisa from Blackpink), but the Korean skincare brand is also delivering some serious results with its Booster Pro X2. The handheld device is a miracle worker for your skin, helping to tone and tighten, while adding brightness and bounce. Use it on its own, or pair it with your skincare products (or over a sheet mask) to boost absorption. The celeb skincare secret is out, and getting that Hollywood glow at home has never been easier.

Spend a few days basking under the glow of a Helio Cure red light panel and a few things become apparent: You’ll feel more awake and alert, while your skin starts looking more refreshed. Helio Cure’s clinical-grade products are said to deliver the industry’s highest density of therapeutic red light wavelengths, which work to help boost collagen production, improve skin tone, and soothe inflammation, while easing sore muscles for post-workout recovery. Easy to use and backed by science, Helio Cure will have you seeing yourself in a whole new light.

Amid concerns over microplastics, it’s understandable that many are looking twice at the tools they use in the kitchen. Some appliances are easier to go plastic-free than others, which is why Caraway’s new coffee maker is so exciting. The brand removed plastic from all of the parts that touch water or coffee. There’s a glass carafe and water reservoir, while the water moves through stainless steel pipes. But that only goes so far if the coffee doesn’t taste good, and Caraway’s coffee maker meets Golden Cup Standards.

We’ve tested plenty of vacuums in our time, and few hold up quite like Shark. Its prices are competitive, but the real selling point is performance: Whether you’re tackling a massive spill or trying to regain control of the pet hair that’s taken over your home, Shark gets the job done. It’s the kind of investment that keeps paying off, year after year.

Air purifiers are an essential part of a healthy home, yet they’re often the most overlooked. If you need a place to start, make it Molekule. Its sleek designs won’t become an eyesore in your living room, while its impressive technology works behind the scenes to help keep your surroundings cleaner. Consider it a breath of fresh air in more ways than one.

Helix mattresses have changed the way we think about getting a good night’s rest. With options backed by sleep experts and designed around different sleeping positions and habits, the brand makes finding your perfect match easier than ever. Not sure what kind of sleeper you are? Its mattress quiz takes the guesswork out of choosing, so you can leave the sheep counting behind.

Macy’s carries hundreds of products, but its own Hotel Collection deserves a moment in the spotlight. From sheets to comforters and bedspreads, the brand brings a posh experience to your bedroom. Think thoughtful craftsmanship, beautiful design, and the kind of luxe lineup that makes getting out of bed feel like a missed opportunity.

Furniture can sometimes feel old and tired, but Sundays is giving everyday essentials a much-needed glow-up. Its couches, beds, chairs, and more feel decidedly modern while still managing to be timeless. And if you just want to upgrade one piece rather than your entire collection (although you might be tempted), everything blends effortlessly with what you already own. Give Sundays a round of applause for making furniture fun again.

A few of our editors own a Rocco fridge, and they all agree: It’s guaranteed to make guests stop and stare. And for good reason. This is no run-of-the-mill beverage cooler; Rocco is a statement piece with room for everything from wine and beer to your favorite nonalcoholic seltzers. It even features smart technology that lets you control the temperature and see what’s inside, though guests might be too busy admiring its good looks to notice.

Riedel helped define how wine should be experienced, and 270 years after it was originally established, Riedel glassware is still the best way to serve any liquid. All of its offerings, from wine glasses to tumblers to decanters, are purpose-built to make your drinks taste better and look better when you serve them.

You might never open your own pizzeria in Brooklyn, but Ooni somehow makes you feel like you’re running the place from behind the counter. The pizza oven brand offers options for everyone, from seasoned pros to first-time pie makers. Its products are easy to use and compact enough to leave plenty of room for all your favorite toppings.

Meraki made a big splash with its Gen 1 espresso machine in 2024. It won over reviewers (us included) with a brilliant semi-automatic functionality, a suite of barista-level features, and a very handsome, compact design. Earlier this year, Meraki launched the Gen 2 version, which adds a 45-step grind range to handle almost any beans and a dual-stage water distribution design, among other new features. In other words, Meraki made an already amazing machine even better. And, maybe just as impressive: Meraki didn’t change the price tag, maintaining a very impressive level of value for an espresso machine this advanced. —Oscar Hartzog

A smartwatch is a great way to meet your fitness goals — until it becomes a distraction. The Fitbit Air has the same appeal as other screen-free fitness trackers, but with a slimmer, more attractive look. The subscription adds more features like an AI-powered fitness coach, but there’s a lot you can get done without it, including workout and sleep tracking. The low-profile bracelet design also makes it a great option where rings or watches might get in the way.

Although the two big guys get most of the running world’s attention, we’re crowning Brooks the best running shoe brand on the market right now. We’ve tested Brooks shoes in all categories, from distance shoes to speed runners to trail shoes, and all of them deliver the same reliable performance, comfort, and long-term durability.

Alo makes great apparel and accessories, but its workout essentials deserve just as much praise. Its premium yoga mats, better known as Warrior Mats, are a standout, with a range of colors to complement your style. They’re also made with easy-to-clean materials, so keeping your mat fresh between sessions is simple — and you can roll it out time and time again.

Hyperice makes massage guns that are perfect for chilling after you’ve given it your all on the trails (or the treadmill), but the brand isn’t afraid to think beyond traditional recovery tools. Its unexpected collaborations, including vibrating sandals designed with Nike, caught our attention for all the right reasons. But if you want to stick to the basics, Hyperice’s massage guns are a pretty great place to start.

After rebranding from Theragun to Therabody, the wellness brand has continued to expand beyond fitness massagers. Their latest tech includes face massagers, red-light therapy devices, and skincare tools, with gadgets that go beyond just being for athletes. But they still excel at making fitness-focused gear, from heated massage guns to compression boots that deliver quick relief.

Lemme might be best known for its co-founder, Kourtney Kardashian Barker, but we’re here to argue that the products deserve their flowers, too. Its lineup of gummies covers everything from sleep and gut health to intimate wellness, so rather than wandering endless supplement aisles trying to figure out what your body needs, you can find targeted options all in one place. They’re tasty enough to make sticking to the suggested serving size the real challenge.

LARQ water bottles may run on the slightly pricier side, but once you see what they can do, they’re worth every penny. Some models come packed with futuristic features like built-in filtration, app connectivity to help track your daily H2O intake, and even self-cleaning technology, while others earn high marks for their sleek looks and durability. Whichever option you choose, it’ll make you think twice about all those other water bottles collecting dust in your cabinet.

Many home gym systems try to invent a new product that supposedly delivers full workout, but Force USA takes a different approach. The brand’s Cornerstone All-In-One setups combine all the most effective elements of a gym into one durable structure. The crown jewel is the C20, a multifunction structure built around a power rack that supports dozens of exercises — all just like the ones you’d hit in the gym. In other words, it’s actually like having a gym at home.

Sometimes scrolling through social media can send you further into your own head, but Headspace is here to do the exact opposite. For as little as $12.99 a month, a subscription gives you access to everything from guided meditation exercises to online therapy, helping you feel a little better with just a few taps.

FitOn turns your phone into a comprehensive wellness guide. The app delivers a massive library of guided workouts, including classes by professional and celebrity coaches, plus content to improve your nutrition and mindfulness practices. Whatever your fitness goal, FitOn is a great way to achieve it (and you don’t even need to leave the house).

Sorting through supplement brands is a mammoth undertaking, but Transparent Labs makes things easier. As the name suggests, the brand is all about candidness, making their formulas as clear and available as possible. They also employ extensive third-party testing and provide Certificates of Analysis (COAs) and certifications from Informed Choice, which give deeper insight to the products than almost any other supplement brand.

Finding Soul is almost as relaxing as taking one of their CBD products. The Austin-based brand offers exceptionally clean cannabis products that are organically grown in the U.S. and subject to thorough third-party testing. They’re also presented in stylish, approachable packages that let consumers know exactly what they’re getting.

LELO has its finger on the pulse — or should we say, button — when it comes to what people actually want from their pleasure products. Its lineup includes sex toys for both men and women, alongside supplements, lubricants, and essentials like condoms, so you can stock up on everything you need without ever having to set foot in a sex shop.

Some products stop you in your tracks, and for me, it was the ridiculously good-looking Porsche x WonderFold Stroller Wagon. I couldn’t get over how something so luxurious could also be so practical, so naturally, I had to take it for a spin. There’s just one small detail: I don’t have kids. Luckily, my brand-new niece was available for a test drive (she didn’t exactly have a say in the matter). At least she’ll have some pretty incredible throwback photos to post one day. From the premium leather seats and headlights to the Porsche-inspired details, spill-proof mat, and upgraded wheels, this stroller doesn’t skimp on the extras. Is it technically for the baby? Absolutely. But let’s be honest: It’s also for the parents who wouldn’t be caught dead in a minivan and have no intention of starting now. —Alexis Mikulski Ruiz

The streaming world has settled down now that most major studios have launched their own services. The best streaming services right now are ones that are comprehensive and customizable. DirecTV wins at both. It offers a wide range of packages for different budgets and viewing needs, and all of them offer a great lineup of live TV channels. Plus, most of them also include complimentary subscriptions to other streaming apps like ESPN Unlimited, HBO Max, Hulu, and Paramount+. If we could only pick one streaming service to use in 2027, it would be DirecTV.

For sports fans, YouTube TV is an exceptional offering. The live-TV streamer offers two packages that both carry dozens of channels, including all major networks showing sports. It also boasts one of the best user interfaces of any live-TV streaming service, and its prices are very reasonable for how much content you get.

Hulu + Live TV delivers all the best content in one package. You get more than 100 live-TV channels, plus all the on-demand content from Hulu, Disney+, and ESPN Select (upgrade for ESPN Unlimited as well). All of this comes in at under $100, making it a great value for anyone looking to keep up with sports, news, and new series.

In terms of original content, HBO Max has yet to be beaten. The platform has one of the deepest libraries of shows that you won’t find anywhere else, including the best Prestige TV lineup with shows like The Sopranos, The Wire, and Game of Thrones. It shows no sign of slowing down, with recent hits like The Pitt, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, The White Lotus, and others reminding us why an HBO Max subscription remains non-negotiable.

Fubo has been a go-to streaming service for sports fans; there are a wide variety of cable networks, plus access to local channels and RSNs to catch games that might not be airing nationally. Beyond sports, you also get access to news, entertainment, and family channels, making it a true replacement for pricier cable packages.

While streaming services were supposed to offer greater freedom and variety, the reality is that many users find themselves toggling through nearly endless rows of options to find what to watch. Criterion brought back the sense of curation to streaming for a relatively affordable monthly price with the Criterion Channel. In addition to on-demand movies, Criterion 24/7 is the streaming service’s all-day livestream, offering a greater sense of mystery and discovery. Leave it to a physical media company to figure out what movie fans really want out of a streaming service.

Of all the streaming services out there, none quite captures the magic of childhood like Disney+. Its library is packed with hundreds of beloved Disney classics, all available on demand without having to dig out an old VHS player. And with live TV options for the adults in the household, there’s something for everyone to enjoy, all for a relatively reasonable monthly price.

Amazon’s latest Fire TV Stick delivers improved performance for ultra-high-definition TVs and a new, more intuitive interface to quickly find the content you want. The new stick also supports Wi-Fi 6 for faster 4K streaming. In short, the Fire TV Stick 4K Plus is all about speed, which is a win in our book.

Tidal has been a go-to hi-fi music streaming service for over a decade, but it’s having a moment. By taking a stance on AI-generated music, including labeling and demonetizing it, the streaming service upped its bona fides as the artist-friendly music streamer. And while it still has some of the highest resolution audio available on streaming and the highest payouts for musicians, it’s managed to do so while keeping the subscription price competitive.

Every year, Rolling Stone editors review hundreds of products and services to separate the standouts from the noise. We put brands to work in the real world, evaluating utility, creativity, design, and performance. We’re looking for companies and products that bring something new to the table: A fresh take on a familiar staple, an unexpected piece of gear we didn’t know we needed, or just something plain cool. Only a select few earn our ultimate seal of approval: A spot in the Rolling Stone Essentials Awards.

Ex-Hungary captain hits out at ‘sobbing’ Andoni Iraola after Dominik Szoboszlai injury comments

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

A former captain of the Hungary national team has criticised Andoni Iraola for his treatment of Dominik Szoboszlai amid an injury concern to the Liverpool midfielder.

The 26-year-old sprained his knee in the first half of the 3-1 victory at Crystal Palace last weekend but continued playing until the 69th minute until he was replaced by Trey Nyoni.

The Reds’ head coach made a thinly-veiled plea afterwards for our number 8 to not feature for his country during this international window, with the Hungarian FA subsequently stating that they’ll defer any decision on his involvement against Georgia and Northern Ireland for as long as possible.

In quotes published by the print edition of Blikk (via Sport Witness), ex-Hungary goalkeeper Janos Csank blasted Iraola for not substituting Szoboszlai at half-time in the win over Palace.

The 80-year-old raged: “I was really annoyed. Szoboszlai went down on the pitch in the first half, and at half-time I was certain he wouldn’t come back on for the second half. Then I saw him standing there on the pitch, with black tape on his leg, his knee.

“Then the manager starts sobbing that he is injured. He should have been substituted straight away; they were leading 1-0. Half-time comes and then he sends him back on injured with his knee bandaged?! I’m losing my mind! I just don’t get it.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

As head coach of Liverpool, the club to whom Szoboszlai is contracted, Iraola is well within his rights to utilise the player as he sees fit, and any decision to send him back out for the second half would surely have been taken in consultation with the medical staff.

We admit that, if that had happened on international duty, we’d be pleading for Marco Rossi to exercise caution and substitute the 26-year-old immediately. Every stakeholder will inevitably shout the loudest in favour of their best interests.

In a similar vein, Reds supporters will hope that the midfielder isn’t deemed fit enough to play for his country in the coming week and instead recover for the Manchester United game the following Sunday, whereas he’ll be striving to prove his fitness in time to face Georgia on Saturday.

Szoboszlai has almost always been available during his three-and-a-bit years as a Liverpool player, although he’s had an injury-hit few weeks of late, having only recently overcome a thigh problem which resurfaced during the win at Brentford last month.

Everyone of a Reds persuasion will face a nervous wait over the next week or so to see whether Iraola will have any fresh injury worries once our season resumes in 10 days’ time. Fingers crossed that everyone on international duty gets back to Merseyside unscathed.

what’s a primate to quantum mechanics? in conversation with daniel birnbaum and jacqui davies

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

at OGR torino, the quantum effect consider how quantum theory travels through cinema, popular culture and contemporary art, from kubrick to schrödinger’s cat.

annalise kamegawa November 12, 2026 9 min

At OGR Torino, The Quantum Effect, an expansive exhibition on art and quantum theory, illustrates the seemingly unknowable principles of quantum mechanics with mirrors, monkeys, and monoliths. To enter the exhibition, one must pass through a series of mirrors that create a dizzying sense of disorientation. Turn a corner, and there’s a projection of those reflective halls being played back to the viewer. It’s a visual collapsing of perception that speaks to the uncertainty of the universe.

In conversation with designboom, curators Daniel Birnbaum and Jacqui Davies discuss how they connected Mark Leckey, Yayoi Kusama, and Tomás Saraceno with Heisenberg’s Uncertainty Principle and Dirac’s Equation. On the interdisciplinary nature of the show, Davies remarks, ‘By placing artworks, experiments and equations in dialogue, the exhibition affirms the validity of each of these languages while resisting their collapse into a single explanatory framework. The show operates – to quote the title of Ryan Gander’s installation – as a ‘school of languages.’ It unfolds across sign systems.’

The curators for the show at OGR Torino consulted Professor Ulf Danielsson, professor of theoretical physics at Uppsala University and Secretary of the Nobel Committee for Physics, to highlight equations important to the field. On this collaboration, Birnbaum notes, ‘Our exhibition tries to stage the co-existence of these symbolic systems without privileging one over the other… [Danielsson] selected the equations that best capture the essence of phenomena such as entanglement, superposition, teleportation, parallel universes or the beginning of quantum computing. Most of us of course cannot read them but Ulf likes to emphasize their beauty.’

Within the exhibition, when staring up at the black monoliths – a wink to Stanley Kubrick’s 2001: A Space Odyssey – these equations appear on the screen as scenes from The Matrix, Star Trek, and footage of Schrödinger’s cat appear from towering heights. Davies, who created the monoliths in a series called Cinematic Blossomings, notes that she, ‘reassembled fragments [she remembers] from cinema, TV and pop culture to make new film-works which resemble ideas from quantum theory,’ in order to create the work.

Although they are not perfect reflections of these scientific principles, this media begins to hint at a shared understanding of the universe. ‘That is what we mean with the quantum effect,’ Davies emphasizes. ‘Even if they remain incomprehensible on an analytical level we are all familiar with some of these phenomena. In The Terminator, Star Trek,  TRON, or Wonder Woman, or David Cronenberg’s The Fly we see bodies shimmer, dematerialize and re-materialize elsewhere. We all grew up with teleportation as something almost normal, happening every day on TV. Other films employ different ideas from quantum physics in imaginative ways.’

On the inclusion of pop culture, Birnbaum acknowledges that, ‘Clearly they have sometimes been simplified or even vulgarized, but we are all familiar with visually extremely powerful imagery,’ citing Kubrick’s film as a prime example. ‘One can find versions of the Many World Interpretation of quantum physics in any number of science fiction movies and speculative works of literary fiction, from Jorge Luis Borges to Philip K Dick or the Matrix. In that way some of the bewildering paradoxes explored by physicists have entered an imaginary that we all share.’

‘Although quantum concepts have become trendy in the artworld in recent years their aesthetic reverberations can be traced throughout the twentieth century.’ Birnbaum remarks, emphasizing that not just pop culture, by the fine arts, have been a site for this exploration. ‘Long before ‘quantum’ became a catchphrase, one finds moments in which science seem to converge in surprising ways. We think Marcel Duchamp’s Large Glass, with it’s exploration of many dimensions, is an example of such convergence.’

Within the exhibition is the persistent fanning out of Duchamp’s influence, pointing to his persistent use of repetition, breaking, and glass. There’s an explicit reference with the inclusion of a photo taken by Man Ray in 1923 of Duchamp holding his work Glissière Contenant un Moulin à Eau (en métaux voisins) (Glider Containing a Water Mill [in Neighbouring Metals], 1913–15). There’s also Sturtevant’s Duchamp descendant l’escalier (1992), a black-and-white photographic interpretation of Duchamp’s painting Nude Descending a Staircase, both of which depict the repetition of a body as it, frame by frame, moves across the canvas.

More subtly, there’s Cerith Wyn Evans’s Phase-shifts )( manifest [after D.T.] (2026), a mobile of cracked windshields, with D.T. in the title standing for ‘delay in time.’ The breaking of glass continues in Mark Leckey’s 2021–22 film, To the Old World (Thank You for the Use of Your Body), in which a man launches his body through a bus stop glass wall, the shards and fragments of which splay into a dark void upon impact. ‘Many of the works explore the possibility of breaking through the glass that separate us from another reality,’ says Davies on the recurrence of the material, ‘One of our curatorial starting points was the novel Locus Solus by Raymond Roussel, an early example of what today is called quantum fiction. In that novel a scientist guides a party through extraordinary, unbelievable things are presented, always behind glass.’

Duchamp’s influence carries on into the primate-heavy works in the exhibition. There’s Rirkrit Tiravanija’s untitled or (the savage detectives) (2025), in which two man-ape figures are crouched on the ground, bearing sticks and staring at a replica of Duchamp’s Peigne, a comb with the inscription, ‘3 or 4 drops from a height have nothing to do with savagery.’ This continues into the liminal space of Ryan Gander’s office space, where School of Languages (2023) and Chronos Kairos 01.01 (2025) are being shown. In a white-walled room, an ape named Brenda cowers underneath a corner desk, only visible when you traverse to the other side of the room and peer underneath. Hanging is a digital clock with the time 88:88, which doesn’t exist on our traditional 24-hour clock.

Once again, there’s a heavy reference to perhaps what can be considered a time before man, the primates that preceded us. With the heavy referencing to Kubrick’s film, one can’t help but think again of that moment where the monolith appears, interrupting linear time with its presence. Here, the curators are calling back to the idea that maybe this straight progression of time, one where we can easily track fish to ape to man, is perhaps more complicated than we may believe.

What emerges in The Quantum Effect is less a definitive explanation of the universe than an invitation to reconsider how we understand it. Like the intricate webs of Tomás Saraceno, whose glowing structures trace paths of seemingly incomprehensible complexity, the exhibition brings together a constellation of references, ideas, and artistic gestures that resist a single point of interpretation. ‘For Tomás they are ciphers of the universe itself. He can talk for ever about string theory in front of these complex creations by spiders,’ says Brinbaum. ‘Good art is never just an illustration of theories. But sometimes one detects this strange convergence between languages.’ From quantum equations and fractured reflections to primates, monoliths, and Duchampian repetitions, the works unsettle familiar understandings of time, perception, and human progress.

Even with the most advanced mathematics and scientific theories, the universe remains fundamentally elusive. Rather than attempting to resolve these uncertainties, the exhibition embraces them, finding in the unknown a space for speculation, connection, and wonder. Ultimately, The Quantum Effect suggests that understanding the world may not be a matter of finding definitive answers, but of remaining open to the possibilities that emerge when art and science meet.

Tomas Saraceno’s Hybrid Webs (2019-25)like the intricate webs of Tomás Saraceno, the exhibition brings together a constellation of references, ideas, and artistic gestures that resist a single point of interpretation

location: OGR Torino, Italy | @ogr_torino

dates: October 31, 2026- February 25, 2027

curators: Daniel Birnbaum and Jacqui Davies

artists: Cerith Wyn Evans, Isa Genzken, Tomás Saraceno, Mark Leckey, Rirkrit Tiravanija, Sturtevant, Emilija Škarnulytė, Ryan Gander, Faustine Studio, and Man Ray

Star of Mauricio Pochettino’s Spurs side reveals how he will change Man Utd

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Former Tottenham star Toby Alderweireld says Mauricio Pochettino is the man to change the mentality at Manchester United and will bring intensity to the team.

Michael Carrick has been dismissed as Red Devils manager, with confirmation coming from the club on Thursday morning.

The club legend’s interim stint from January to the end of last season could scarcely have gone better, but his permanent spell in charge has been disastrous, with United winning just one of their 10 Premier League games this campaign.

Pochettino is now expected to be announced as Carrick’s replacement, with the USA manager set to leave his role and return to the Premier League where he has previously managed Chelsea, Southampton and Spurs.

His stint in charge of Tottenham was the most significant of his career, leading the club to a Champions League and second-place finish in the Premier League.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

Alderweireld was a big part of that success, playing for Spurs from 2015-21, and he has high hopes for his former boss at Old Trafford.

‘He will make players fit, that’s for sure,’ the Belgian told talkSPORT. ‘Second of all he brings intensity. It’s not like everyone is playing their own game, he wants a team that’s going to be on the front foot. He wants to play pressure and having the ball as well.

‘A clear idea, intensity and players being fit, I think that’s the way he likes to work.’

Alderweireld reckons Pochettino is going to want the club to bend to his will to some extent, expecting the Argentine to insist on influence in the transfer market.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

‘The summer, maybe the winter as well, the transfer window is going to be the players that he wants,’ said the former defender.

‘Also the facilities are going to be built also to the things that he wants. Making players fit, food-wise, gym-wise, everything needs to be how he wants it, that’s a big part of his philosophy.

‘Then he’ll want to buy the players. Maybe not always the biggest names, biggest talents, but guys with desire to win, desire to become better, desire to run. I think if you see the players he bought for Spurs, that’s thing number one, the desire to become better, the desire to win games, wins duels, to run.

‘That’s why I think Pochettino is the man for Man United, to change things around. In the past couple of transfer windows they’ve bought good players, good quality but they miss some chemistry, they miss the intensity and I think Pochettino will bring that.
